## E23 — training: more weight on people's games for the value net (offline only, READY Oct 17, 9:40 PM Toronto; launch needs the lead: `nohup bash data/orchestrator/jobs/e23-run.sh > data/orchestrator/jobs/e23.log 2>&1 &`, pid to jobs/e23.pid; workers can't run it)
- **Why:** gap 1. The value net is too sure of a late lead against people (turn 75+ bias +0.136, v ≥ 0.5 claims +0.87 but realises +0.70 on the holdout). E16 showed it is not target noise. People's games are a small share of what it learns: data/human/train/value is 126,644 positions (13,554 at turn 75+) against ~730k per self-play round (144k at turn 75+). At HUMAN_REPEAT 4 over 6 rounds that is ~10% of positions and ~6% of turn-75+ ones.
- **Hypothesis:** listing the human value folder 16 (~31% of positions, ~20% late) or 32 times (~48%, ~33% late) lowers the turn-75+ bias and holdout MSE; the risk is overfitting the 3,212 human games.
- **Design:** E16's m0 repeated (from round 66, rounds 61-66, 4 epochs, lr 3e-4, ema 0.9997, seed 67, 3M cap); arms h16, h32 and h4 (baseline rerun, the human folder was rebuilt since E16). Controller data/orchestrator/jobs/e23-run.sh (pid in e23.pid, log e23.log) starts an arm only in the loop's match/benchmark step, holding gpu.lock; nets copied to data/orchestrator/e23/<arm>/net.npz. Scoring here: scratch/training/long_games.py and calibrate on data/human/holdout/value (545 games).
- **Metric / verdict:** adopt only if turn-75+ bias falls by ≥0.03 and holdout MSE by ≥0.005 against h4, paired by game, with self-play val policy no worse. Then one production round with HUMAN_REPEAT at the chosen value, after E14 closes (round 72), gated as usual.
- **Revert:** nothing deployed. Kill with scripts/vm/orchestrator/kill_job.sh e23 --yes.
- **Side check, Oct 17, 9:55 PM (training): can an inference-time fix do it instead? No.** Per-turn-bucket fit z ≈ a·v + b on data/human/train/value (126,644 positions), scored on the holdout (22,308 positions). Round 66: holdout MSE 0.5841 raw → 0.5794 affine (−0.005), 0.5853 scale-only; turn 75–90 0.522 → 0.503. Round 70: 0.5795 → 0.5753; turn 75–90 0.504 → 0.489. Slopes are 1.04–1.11 in every bucket (never < 1) with a negative offset of −0.08 to −0.14: across the whole range the net is not too steep, it is shifted up, and the shift is the same at turns 20–75 and only a bit larger late. A monotone per-turn transform can't change sign accuracy (turn 75–90: 81.9% r66, 82.7% r70, against 86% at turns 40–75), and a near-constant shift changes little inside a search. So the late error is mostly about which leads people overturn, not about the scale: it needs data (this experiment), not a recalibration layer. Not building one. Script: scratch/training/late_fix.py NET TRAIN HOLDOUT (~4 min). Side result for E14: round 70 (people's moves x3) cuts holdout MSE 0.5841 → 0.5795 and turn 75–90 bias +0.162 → +0.149 against round 66.

## E31 — training: one epoch per round instead of four (CLOSED Oct 18, 10:35 AM Toronto: no strength change, faster, kept)
- **Subsystem:** self-play/training (after E27; before SELFPLAY_GAMES 7500).
- **Why:** rounds 74-79 vs round 73 pooled: 8,034 of 16,000 = 50.2% ± 0.8%: a real plateau, not a detection problem. In every round 72-80 the val loss is flat from epoch 1 to 4 (e.g. r80 val policy 0.881 → 0.882, value 0.378 → 0.379) while train policy falls 0.871 → 0.855: epochs 2-4 fit the window, not the game. Each row is reused ~18-24 times over its 6 rounds in the window (E4's estimate ×4 epochs); KataGo's main run used each row about 4 times (954M training samples over 241M rows, Wu 2019, arxiv.org/abs/1902.10565).
- **Change:** TRAIN_EPOCHS=1 in scripts/vm/workers.env, safe_restart_training.sh. Everything else unchanged (lr, ema, window 6, 4M sample).
- **Hypothesis:** the same val loss at a quarter of the training time (13 → ~4 min per round) and a gate score vs best at or above the 4-epoch rounds, because less memorisation of stale rounds.
- **Metrics / verdict after 3 rounds:** (1) epoch-1 val policy/value within 0.005 of the previous rounds' epoch-4 values (0.879-0.891 / 0.371-0.403, the val set moves); (2) gate score vs best, mean of 3 rounds, compared with rounds 74-79's 50.2%; (3) live value MSE no worse than 0.01; (4) training minutes per round. A promotion is the real signal.
- **Revert:** TRAIN_EPOCHS=4, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Oct 18, 9:05 AM (training): DEPLOYED.** TRAIN_EPOCHS=1 set in scripts/vm/workers.env. No new restart queued: the two already waiting (E23 fix and E30/E32) fire at round 81's match, after round 81 has trained with 4 epochs. So round 82 is the first 1-epoch round; rounds 82-84 decide. Check the loop's args show `--epochs 1` after the restart.
- **Oct 18, 9:30 AM (training): LIVE since the 9:10 AM restart** (one restart only; the loop's args show `--epochs 1`). Round 82, the first 1-epoch round: val policy 0.880, value 0.383 (inside the 0.879-0.891 / 0.371-0.403 range), 182 s of training against 4 x ~186 s before. Its match began 9:25 AM. Rounds 82-84 decide.
- **Oct 18, 10:35 AM (training): verdict NO STRENGTH CHANGE, faster; KEPT, watch late MSE.** (1) Val loss the same: r82 0.880/0.383, r83 0.879/0.385, r84 0.880/0.373 (all in range). (2) Gate vs r73: r82 49.7% (2000 games), r83 49.8% (2000), r84 47.0% (1000, futility stop); pooled 2459/5000 = 49.2% ± 1.4% against 4-epoch rounds 74-79's 50.2% ± 0.8%: no difference within noise, no promotion. So the plateau is not from memorising the window. (3) Live MSE 0.577, 0.578, 0.579 (r81 0.575; limit 0.585), but late-game MSE rose each round: 0.424, 0.426, 0.430 (4-epoch rounds 0.420-0.424). Plausible cause: one pass sees the human value data in --extra-data a quarter as often. (4) Training 182-191 s vs ~12 min: a round is now training ~3 min + match ~10 min, about as long as self-play of 5000 games (round 84 waited 50 s for its self-play), against ~22 min before: ~1.6x more rounds and self-play data per hour. Kept for the speed. **Revert trigger:** late MSE ≥ 0.435 or overall ≥ 0.585 at any round, then TRAIN_EPOCHS=2 (not 4). Closed.
- **Oct 18, 10:47 AM (training): footnote.** Round 84's 47.0% is under the 48% floor, so round 85 trains from round 73 (10:41 AM), not from round 84. Two things outside E31 slowed rounds 83-85, sent to efficiency: (a) the matches got about 2x slower per move since the self-play queue (301be28) went live: thinking 137 ms per move in round 81's match, then 212, 255, 280 ms in rounds 82-84; 2000 games took 297 s of play in round 81, 525-551 s in rounds 82-83. (b) Round 83's training failed twice (9:41 and 9:45 AM) with "File is not a zip file" on the GPU host, about 7 min lost, likely a self-play shard read while still being written. Round 85 also waited 10 min for its self-play. The E31 verdict stands: the gate is a head-to-head score, so slower matches don't bias it.
- **Oct 18, 2:20 PM (training): REVERT TRIGGER HIT, TRAIN_EPOCHS=2.** Late live MSE r90 0.435, r91 0.438, r92 0.439, r93 0.440 (overall 0.582-0.584). Each 1-epoch chain from r73 climbs about 0.003 per round: r85 0.427 → r88 0.433 (reset to r73), r89 0.430 → r93 0.440. Not a harder live set: r73 on today's set (24,089 positions, 614 games) gives 0.574 overall, late 0.420, the same as before. Gate unchanged (r85-r93 47.3-50.6%, no promotion), and live bots still run r73, so nothing live was affected. TRAIN_EPOCHS=2 set in scripts/vm/workers.env at 2:18 PM; efficiency's queued restart (E36, waiting on round 94) picks it up, so round 95 is the first 2-epoch round. **Watch:** late MSE of rounds 95-97 should hold at or under ~0.43 instead of climbing; if it still climbs, the cause is the --train-from latest chain rather than the epochs, and TRAIN_EPOCHS=4 is the next step.

## E32 — efficiency: match games as a live queue the hosts draw from (CLOSED Oct 18, 9:30 AM Toronto: works, kept)
- **Why:** each 1000-game block waits for its slowest host. Round 80's first block (split by self-play speed): median host 318 s, slowest 407 s, wall 6.8 min. Even identical t2d VMs with 57 games each ranged 283-372 s, so a fixed split (E30's measured speeds included) keeps a ~15-20% tail.
- **Change:** the coordinator still plays its own share by measured speed; all other games are one queue. Each host holds one game per process and gets the next when one comes back (new `evaluate --tasks-stream`, unordered, indexed results). Games a failed host didn't return go back on the queue, the rest are played here. `--tasks-stdin` kept so a still-running old coordinator keeps working. Play unchanged: a game is a pure function of its task (test: results identical to local play, also via a 2-process host).
- **Metric / verdict:** 'evaluation games by host' after the restart: all hosts within about one game time of each other (~20-80 s); wall per 1000 games near the median host, about 5.3-5.5 min vs 6.6-6.8 min. Two rounds.
- **Deploy:** the training restart already waiting (from E30) picks it up at round 81's match. **Revert:** git revert the commit, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Oct 18, 9:16 AM (efficiency): DEPLOYED, first result.** The training restart fired at 9:10 AM and round 81's match runs E30 and E32 together. First 1000 games: 9:10:21-9:15:17 AM, **4.9 min** (round 80: 6.8 min, round 79: 6.6). All 21 worker hosts finished within 279-297 s of the start (round 80: 283-407 s). The coordinator's own share (14 games) was done at 82 s, so it sits idle for about 200 s: a small next fix is to let it draw from the queue as well. The two 2-core hosts the lead asked about (3.17.59.49, 18.227.92.226) played 17 and 19 games, the same as the third 2-core host (3.19.209.181, 18): no sign of leftover E18 processes slowing them (I can't ssh to check directly). Verdict after round 82's match.
- **Oct 18, 9:22 AM (efficiency): second block, works so far.** Games 1001-2000: 9:15:17-9:20:04 AM, **4.8 min**; hosts 270-287 s. The coordinator's share fixed itself as E30 intended: its measured speed from block 1 gave it 47 games, done at 201 s, so no code change is needed there. Round 81's whole 2000-game match took 9.7 min (round 80: 6.4 min per 1000). E29 → E32 together: 14-16 → 4.8 min per 1000 games. Final verdict after round 82's match confirms it.
- **Oct 18, 9:30 AM (efficiency): verdict for E30 and E32 together: works, keep.** Two blocks at 4.9 and 4.8 min per 1000 (round 80: 6.8, 6.6 and 6.4 per 1000), every worker host within 262-298 s, the coordinator 201 s in block 2. About 30% faster than E29 alone and 3x faster than Oct 17 (14-16 min). Closed. **Next bottleneck:** round 82's self-play (5000 games) ran 8:56-9:09:51 AM, 13.8 min, while sharing the fleet with round 81's match. With E31 (training ~4 min) a round is now ~4 min training + ~10 min match for 2000 games, so self-play is as long as a round and at 7,500 games would set the pace. ## E33 — efficiency: self-play as a live queue of 8-game parts (CLOSED Oct 18, 12:10 PM Toronto: works, kept; live since round 85, a stop bug fixed at 11:07 AM)
- **Why:** self-play is now as long as a round (E31 cut training to ~4 min). Round 82 alone: 13.1 min for 5000 games, the fleet waiting ~2.3 min on its last hosts; host speeds vary 7.0-9.2 s a game on identical t2d VMs and aren't stable from round to round, so a fixed split can't fix the tail. The old scheme (fixed split, stealing every 3 min, duplicates, a second wave for lost shards) also leaves idle machines waiting for the next look.
- **Change:** the round is one queue of 8-game parts (625 for 5000 games). Every machine, the coordinator too, runs `selfplay --parts-stream` and gets the next part whenever a process frees up; each reported part is copied back at once and merged into its 64-game shard when complete. Once the queue is empty an idle machine also plays a part one other machine is playing (first copy wins), and the rest are stopped when all parts are in. A failed or unreachable host's parts go back on the queue. The coordinator starts playing while the hosts are prepared. Play unchanged: a game is a pure function of its seed, and parts merge to the same shard (tests; end-to-end check with the real worker and round 73's net: shards identical to one local run).
- **Metric / verdict:** self-play minutes for 5000 games from 'self-play: N parts' to 'self-play complete' in round_NNN/prefetch.log, and 'self-play games by host' (all machines finishing within about one part, ~1-4 min). Before: 13.1 min (round 82, alone with training), 13.8 min (round 82 sharing the fleet with a match). Two rounds.
- **Revert:** git revert the commit, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Oct 18, 11:15 AM (efficiency): first two rounds; the queue works, a stop bug fixed.** Round 85: all 5000 games in 16.4 min (10:17-10:33 AM); every host's last part ended within 41 s of the others (round 82: 2.3 min tail). Round 86: 17.9 min (10:41-10:59 AM). Both ran while a match shared the fleet the whole time. Per-game speed on the workers was already ~55% slower from round 83 on, before the queue (t2d hosts 7.5 → 11-13 s a game per process): E31's short training puts self-play on top of the match. So 16-18 min is not a slowdown from the queue, and round 82's 13.1/13.8 min is no longer a fair baseline. **Bug:** after all parts were in, the coordinator's own worker hung on its stop (its pool deadlocked in terminate) and the round waited until the loop ended it 8 min later, leaving the worker behind with 8 processes. No round time was lost (the match was longer), and the data was complete. Fixed in 87d393d (the worker kills its pool on SIGTERM; a test hangs without the fix), landed, and live for the workers from round 87 (the 11:07 AM restart). The coordinator's 20 s kill backstop goes live at the next loop restart. **Verdict after round 88:** check that 'self-play complete' and 'self-play games by host' appear within a minute of the last part.
- **Oct 18, 12:10 PM (efficiency): verdict, kept.** Round 87: 18.1 min (11:29-11:47 AM), hosts' last parts within 90 s of each other (995-1083 s), 'self-play complete' 2 s after the last stop: the stop fix works. Round 88: 18.8 min (11:50 AM-12:09 PM), hosts within ~2 min (994-1125 s). No 'stopped with parts unfinished', no fallback games here, no tmp files left in round 85-88's data. Both rounds shared the fleet with a match nearly the whole time, so self-play minutes alone aren't the measure; the whole round is: 21-22 min per round with the queue (rounds 86→87→88, when nothing failed) vs 21-24 min before (rounds 81-83). The gain is the end of the tail and robustness, not raw speed. **Training's questions (10:48 AM):** (1) the match's slower moves (137 → 212-280 ms) come from self-play and the match sharing every machine since E31 shortened training; rounds whose self-play had already finished (81, 85) ran at 137-139 ms. It's a split of the same CPUs, not lost time. (2) Round 83's 'not a zip file': half-written shard_*.npz.tmp.npz files that the old split's copy-back left in round_083/data and round_084/data (4 files, 9:30-10:12 AM, before the queue went live). The queue copies finished parts by name and merges locally, so none since; training's 0ebfb80 skips such files anyway.

## E34 — openhex: OpenHex rolls behave like plain dice; does the net's balanced-dice training cost strength? (CLOSED Oct 18, 11:40 AM Toronto: no measurable cost; plain odds from the first roll, kept)
- **Wording (owner, Oct 18, 11:20 AM):** OpenHex's own game specification says 1v1 uses "Dice Mode: Balanced" (4-player: Random). The rolls don't behave like a 36-card deck: all 56 saved games (4356 rolls) contain a roll colonist's deck cannot make (median: the 8th roll); in the first 23 rolls 49 of 55 games show a total more often than a deck holds it (plain dice: 82% of games, a deck: never); per-game chi-square vs 2d6 9.5 (simulated plain 9.9, balanced 3.4; the lead's rerun on 57 games: 9.6 vs 9.9). So either OpenHex's balancing is much weaker than colonist's, or it isn't applied in bot games; our own saved game states also say "diceMode": "balanced" (121 of 121 files), so the setting is on and the rolls still look plain. We treat the rolls as plain dice. The site's guide (data/openhex/discovery/js_app.js, "2. Game Specification") names the mode but doesn't describe the algorithm, and has no contact address, email or Discord link.
- **Hypothesis:** the net, trained only on balanced dice, plays worse on OpenHex because its dice input there (balanced flag 0, deck counts frozen) is unfamiliar.
- **Metric:** round 73 on plain dice, policy only (no search, where the input matters most): balanced-looking dice input vs the input the client feeds. 1000 games (500 seed pairs, seats swapped).
- **Result (Oct 18, 11:15 AM, details in E20's 11:15 AM entry):** balanced-looking input 51.2% ± 3.1% (95%) over 1000 games: no measurable cost (under about 3% with no search). Sensitivity on 55 OpenHex games (4951 decisions): top move changes in 6.8% of positions, mean value shift 0.06; the empty dice block (dice=None, what `evaluate --plain-dice` and `dice="random"` give) is much further off (20%, 0.16), so we keep the dice object.
- **Change (lead's request, Oct 18, 11:05 AM), landed 71c1563:** the follower's dice start in plain mode, so the search uses plain odds from the first roll instead of the deck's odds until the first impossible roll (about the 8th). The net then sees the same kind of input the 1000-game test measured. Pushed to the OpenHex VM at 11:30 AM with a pause; the client restarts after its current game (scratch/openhex/restart_after_game.py, job openhex-restart).
- **Revert:** git revert 71c1563, push_vm.py, restart the VM client between games.

## E35 — training: self-play and the gate on colonist's number layout (the official spiral) instead of random numbers (PROPOSED Oct 18, 12:05 PM Toronto; waits for the lead to slot it around E23)
- **Finding (openhex's tip, checked on our own captures):** 228 distinct colonist boards from the live bots' captures (`captures/*/bridge-*.json`, converted with convert()): **228 of 228 follow the official number spiral** (5,2,6,3,8,10,9,12,11,4,8,10,9,4,5,6,3,11, skipping the desert), started from any of the 6 corners in either direction (12 variants, 18-19 boards each; desert in the centre 12/228). So no 6 ever touches an 8 and no equal numbers touch (0 of 228 boards). Our self-play (`rules.new_game`, number_placement='random') has a 6 next to an 8 on 86% of boards (mean 1.48 pairs) and equal numbers touching on 90%. Pairs that never occur on colonist but would ~50-225 times on 228 random boards: 6-8, 8-9, 6-6, 8-8, 2-12, 2-3, 4-12, 10-12 and every equal pair. rules.py's docstring said "not confirmed, boards look random": they aren't.
- **Exposure now:** 35% of self-play games start from people's openings (real colonist boards, so spiral already); the other 65% and **every gate game** are on random-number boards. Catanatron's official_spiral always starts at one corner and direction, but train.py applies the 12 board symmetries, which are exactly colonist's 12 variants, so a fixed start is enough.
- **Hypothesis:** training on the boards live play actually uses moves the value head toward live conditions (no stacked 6/8 hot spots, which make early leads more decisive on random boards); live value error falls, mainly opening/middle, maybe late. Gate scores are on the new boards for both nets, so stay comparable within the experiment.
- **Change:** one line, `rules.new_game` default number_placement "random" → "official_spiral" (self-play fresh games, datagen, the gate's evaluate all use it). Landing it is deploying it: the hosts run ~/colonist-ai's code from the next round.
- **Metric / verdict:** live value MSE overall and by phase (now 0.579; opening 0.886, middle ~0.50, late 0.430) over 3 rounds trained on mostly-spiral data; gate score vs best (no regression vs 49-50%); self-play value MSE on spiral boards. Then live win rate over 100+ games if a net promotes.
- **Revert:** git revert, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Script:** data/orchestrator/scratch/training/colonist_boards.py (n boards as argument).
- **Rest of the board checked (Oct 18, 11:55 AM):** over 4,279 distinct colonist boards, everything except the numbers already matches ours. Port positions: the same 9 spots on every board, ours too. Port types: shuffled (3,718 distinct orders; ours 490 of 500). Resource tiles: random on both (same-resource neighbours 5.86 per board vs ours 5.84-5.97; desert in the centre 5.0% vs 4.6-6.2%). So the numbers are the only board difference; E35 stays a one-line change. Script: data/orchestrator/scratch/training/colonist_ports.py.

## E36 — efficiency: each self-play process keeps 32 games in play, refilling from the queue (LANDING Oct 18, 2:10 PM Toronto)
- **Change:** a --parts-stream process used to play one 8-game part at a time; at each part's end its network batches shrank to a game or two. Now each process keeps up to 32 games in play (selfplay.STREAM_GAMES) and starts its next part as games end; the coordinator gives each machine 5 parts per process (distributed.STREAM_PARTS, was 1). Parts stay 8 games; done() per part as before.
- **Measured (busy coordinator, round_073 vs 070/071/AB:2/F, 400 sims, full_rate .25, fast 24, three runs at once):** 64 seeds (7,000,032+) in one process: one part at a time 14.91 s/game, 16 in play 14.39 (-3.5%), **32 in play 13.82 (-7.3%)**. 32 seeds: 15.40 vs 15.10 with 16 in play. Same games: every move, state and result identical; 2 of 3.4M policy targets differ by one float16 step (batch rounding); 32 seeds merged to the exact bytes of a 32-game shard.
- **Hypothesis:** self-play minutes per round fall ~5-7% (less on the tail).
- **Metric / verdict:** self-play minutes for the same game count over the next 3 rounds vs rounds 80-85; watch the round's tail (a slow machine holds up to 5 parts per process; idle machines copy parts only one machine plays) and worker memory (a 32-game process ~360 MB).
- **Revert:** git revert the commit (or set STREAM_GAMES = 8 and STREAM_PARTS = 1); takes effect next round.
- **Baseline (Oct 18, 2:25 PM):** rounds 80-85 swing too much (13.5-20.4 min) to compare against. Rounds 87-94 are steady: 5,000 games, 18.3-18.7 min from start to last part (mean 18.5), tail after 95% of games 0.2-0.9 min (mean 0.6). Self-play runs alongside training and the gate match, and a round takes ~21 min, so self-play is only just off the critical path today. The gain will show as room for more games per round (the planned 7,500). The restart is queued on round 94; round 95 started on the old code, so **round 96 is the first clean round**. Measured with scratch sp_minutes.py (shard file times vs train.log's start line).
- **Round 96 (Oct 18, 3:15 PM), first on the new code: slower overall, cause found and fixed.** Each 4-core VM played 30% faster (0.26-0.27 → 0.34-0.38 games/s; fleet total 5.04 → 5.70 games/s with 2 VMs, 35.223.45.100 and 35.237.216.168, unreachable over SSH). But self-play took 21.2 min (95% at 20.1) vs 18.5, because 6,640 games were played for 5,000. With 5 parts per process held, the queue ran dry a third of the way in, and every free slot then took a copy of a part a busy machine held. Fix landed (b3f6a3f, 3:55 PM): a machine takes a copy only once it holds less than a part per process. Restart queued (applies about 4:20 PM); **round 99 is the first round with the fix.** Expected: about 5,000-5,300 games played, self-play about 15-16 min.
- **Round 98 (Oct 18, 4:15 PM), first round with the fix (the restart came at 3:59 PM, before round 98's self-play):** 5,216 games played for 5,000 (round 97: 7,024; round 96: 6,640). Self-play 15.4 min to the last part (95% at 14.3, tail 1.1), against 20.7 for round 97 and 18.5 for rounds 87-94: **17% less than the old baseline**, with two VMs (10.142.0.2 and 10.142.0.6) absent. ## E23 — LAUNCHED (lead, Oct 18, 2:45 PM Toronto). Controller data/orchestrator/jobs/e23-run.sh started detached through data/orchestrator/scratch/lead/launch_sh.py (pid 1224833, log jobs/e23.log). Arms h16, h32, h4 start one at a time in the first 2 minutes of a round's promotion match; the loop waits for gpu.lock (a283493). First arm expected at round 95's match (about 3:00 PM). Training scores the arms when data/orchestrator/e23/<arm>/net.npz appear and writes the verdict.

- **Preview, Oct 18, 4:10 PM Toronto (training): h16 and h32 against E16's m0 (same design at repeat 4, older human folder), h4 still to run.** Holdout data/human/holdout/value now 615 games / 24,129 positions (2,428 at turn 75+); paired by game, 2,000 bootstrap draws over games. m0: MSE 0.5805, turn 75+ bias +0.154. h16: MSE −0.0024 [−0.0049, +0.0001], turn 75+ bias −0.017 [−0.021, −0.013]. h32: MSE −0.0016 [−0.0052, +0.0019], bias −0.027 [−0.033, −0.021]. Neither meets the bar (bias ≥0.03 AND MSE ≥0.005 lower); h32 nearly makes the bias half but not the MSE half. For scale, round 73 (production) against m0: MSE −0.0068, bias −0.009, so production is already better on error than either arm. Expect a fail unless h4 comes out clearly worse than m0. Script: scratch/training/e23_score.py name=net ... (first is the baseline, ~2 min per net).
- **VERDICT, Oct 18, 4:45 PM Toronto (training): FAIL, CLOSED. Nothing deployed.** h4 finished 4:35 PM. Holdout 619 games / 24,289 positions (2,449 at turn 75+), paired by game, 2,000 bootstrap draws. h4: MSE 0.5799, turn 75+ bias +0.158 (m0 vs h4: MSE +0.0003, bias −0.003, so the rerun matches E16's m0). h16 vs h4: MSE −0.0022 [−0.0048, +0.0002], bias −0.020 [−0.024, −0.017]. h32 vs h4: MSE −0.0014 [−0.0052, +0.0022], bias −0.030 [−0.037, −0.025]. Bar: bias ≥0.03 AND MSE ≥0.005 lower. h16 misses both; h32 just meets the bias half, but its error is not lower (interval spans zero), so it only shifts values down without predicting people's results better. Round 73 (production) vs h4: MSE −0.0066 [−0.0112, −0.0024], bias −0.012: production is already better than every arm. Self-play policy not checked (the arms fail before that matters). Conclusion: more weight on people's games is not the lever for the late gap; HUMAN_REPEAT stays 4. The PC's GPU is free for E40 when the lead says go.

No restart needed: each round's early self-play is a new process, and remote hosts get the code synced for each job. Gate matches from round 95's onward may mix boards; both nets play the same boards, so scores stay fair. ## E37 — training: an 18-round data window instead of 6 (PLANNED Oct 18, 3:00 PM Toronto; go/no-go at the 9 PM run)
- **Why:** round 73 has held for 21 rounds (74-94: 47-50.6%). E11b: the net is data-limited, not capacity-limited. E31: more passes over the same 6-round window do nothing. The 14 rounds outside the window come from nets of the same strength as the 6 inside, so they are unused data with no staleness cost. KataGo's shuffle step sizes the window as a growing function of all rows generated (defaults keep every row; the official runs 0.4 per row with a 0.65-power taper, lightvector/KataGo python/shuffle.py); ours is fixed at 6 rounds (train.sh --window 6 --train-sample 4000000).
- **Change:** train.sh --window 18 --train-sample 12000000, TRAIN_EPOCHS 2, safe_restart_training.sh. Nothing else.
- **Hypothesis:** gate score vs round 73 rises above the 47-50.6% band; a promotion is the signal.
- **Metric / verdict:** mean gate score over 3 rounds vs rounds 74-94; val policy/value loss; training minutes per round (expect ~20 at 2 epochs: a 3.8M-position epoch takes ~190 s on the PC's GPU), which puts training on the round's critical path.
- **Revert:** --window 6 --train-sample 4000000, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Order:** after E31's revert watch (rounds 95-97). E35's data change runs alongside; its metric is live MSE by phase, E37's is the gate score.
- **Cost and a correction (training, Oct 18, 3:45 PM):** the planned 12M-position sample can't run. The GPU PC loads at most 4M positions a round (loop.py --train-max-positions, set for its ~9 GB of memory). Rounds 79-96 hold 12.1M positions (about 670k a round, 5000 games) plus 138k human ones, about 15 GB once loaded (about 1.2 KB a position). Today's 6-round window already holds 4.57M, so the PC loads a random 87% of it. What fits: a window of 18 with the same 4M cap. Each round then trains on a different 4M drawn by shard from 18 rounds (the draw is seeded by the round), so consecutive rounds share about 30% of their positions instead of about 75%. Over a 2-epoch chain from the previous net, that is the extra distinct data E37 wants. Training time is unchanged (about 7-8 min at 2 epochs: about 50 s loading, about 185 s an epoch); the first round copies about 0.8 GB of older folders to the PC once. The human folder's share would fall from 12% to 4.4% at HUMAN_REPEAT 4, so set HUMAN_REPEAT 12 to keep it at 12%. Each round's newest data falls from about 15% to about 5% of what is loaded.
- **Ready (landed 0b57fac, no change until set):** train.sh reads TRAIN_WINDOW. To go live: in ~/colonist-ai/scripts/vm/workers.env set TRAIN_WINDOW=18 and HUMAN_REPEAT=12, then safe_restart_training.sh. Revert: remove both lines, safe_restart_training.sh. A true 12M sample needs a machine with about 16 GB free, or smaller stored positions; not planned.
- **E39 changes E37's premise (training, Oct 18, 3:20 PM):** from round 97 each round trains from round 73, not from the previous net, so a wider window no longer adds up across rounds through the chain. E37 still gives each round a different 4M draw from 18 rounds; if the lead says go, measure it the same way (gate score over 3 rounds).
- **GO, as a true 12M sample on the VM (lead, Oct 18, 8:25 PM; set by training about 8:40 PM Toronto):** landed 09634b3 (train.sh reads TRAIN_SAMPLE and TRAIN_MAX_POSITIONS, 4M each unless set). workers.env: TRAIN_WINDOW=18, TRAIN_SAMPLE=12000000, TRAIN_MAX_POSITIONS=12000000, HUMAN_REPEAT=12; safe_restart_training.sh queued. Round 106's match had already begun, so the restart fires at round 107's match and **round 108 is the first E37 round** (about 9:15 PM). E39 (train from best) stays on. Expect about 8.4 GB loaded on the VM (29 GB) and about 300 s an epoch, so 10-12 min of training a round; the first round also copies the 12 older folders to the VM once. If the VM fails, the coordinator's fallback loads the same 12M (about 8.4 GB of its 24 GB free at 8:40 PM).
- **Verdict on:** mean gate score over rounds 108-110 against rounds 97-105 under E39 (47.6-49.7%); val losses; minutes from "training on" to the gate (VM at 6 rounds/4M: about 295 s). Under 49% mean: say so, and the lead goes back to the chain (--train-from latest).
- **Revert:** remove TRAIN_WINDOW, TRAIN_SAMPLE, TRAIN_MAX_POSITIONS and HUMAN_REPEAT from workers.env, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Round 108, the first E37 round (training, Oct 18, 10:05 PM Toronto):** live and clean. 33 data folders, 11.40M train / 0.60M validation positions (119,960 games), on the VM, no fallback, no GPU waits. Epochs 297/296 s (4M: 101-105 s); "training on" to the gate 14.6 min (was about 5). Val value 0.408, policy 0.869, accuracy 77.6% (r106-107 at 4M: 0.412-0.421, 0.885-0.887, 76.0%; the validation set is now drawn from 18 rounds, so only roughly comparable). Gate: 50.3% at 4000 games, a near miss, so 4000 more: **50.09% ± 1.05% over 8000**, kept round 73. That is the best score since round 96 (E39 rounds 97-107: 47.6-49.7%), but within noise of it. Live MSE 0.578, by phase 0.885 / 0.506 / 0.424 (r107: 0.886 / 0.510 / 0.428). The round took 46 min (training 15 + an 8000-game match 31). Rounds 109-110 next; verdict about 11:30 PM.
- **Memory on the VM (efficiency, Oct 18, 10:20 PM Toronto):** watched gpu-train-1 every 20 s through round 109's load and first epoch (10:06-10:17 PM, scratch mem_watch.py over ssh). Peak RAM used **10.5 GB of 29.4 GB** (36%), steady at 10.4-10.5 GB; the training process 9.6 GB; GPU memory 0.8 GB of the T4's 15 GB. Expected 8.4 GB for the positions alone; the rest is the process and torch. Room for about 2.5x more positions. Round 109: 11.41M train positions, epoch 1 305 s (round 108: 297/296 s; 4M rounds: 99-105 s, so time grows in line with positions).
- **Rounds 109-110 (training, Oct 18, 11:50 PM Toronto):** r109: epochs 305/304 s, val value 0.402 / policy 0.865 / acc 77.8%; gate 50.0% at 4000 (near miss), **49.85% ± 1.06% over 8000**; live MSE 0.580, by phase 0.886 / 0.509 / 0.428. r110: epochs 309/305 s, val value 0.400 / policy 0.867 / acc 77.7%; gate 51.0% at 4000 (near miss), **50.51% ± 1.05% over 8000**; live MSE 0.581, by phase 0.885 / 0.510 / 0.429. Both kept round 73 (37 rounds without a promotion). No GPU waits, no fallback.
- **VERDICT, Oct 18, 11:50 PM Toronto: KEPT.** Mean gate over rounds 108-110 is **50.15%** (24,000 games), above the 49% bar, against 49.23% for rounds 103-107 (20,000 games; 48.43-49.62%). About +0.9 points, roughly 2 standard errors; no promotion yet. Every E37 round went to a near miss (50.0-51.0% at 4000), which no 4M round did. Live error unchanged (0.578-0.581; late 0.424-0.429 vs 0.428). Cost: training about 15 min a round instead of 5, and near misses double the match, so a round takes about 47 min instead of about 30. E37's settings stay in workers.env. Next lever (lead's call, one change at a time): VM RAM allows about 2.5x more positions, e.g. a wider window or more self-play games a round.

## E38 — training: the promotion gate at 51% with longer matches (owner, Oct 18, 3:02 PM; LIVE from round 96's match)
- **Why:** rounds 74-95 all scored 47-50.6% against round 73. A real 1% gain can't show in a 2000-game match (± 2.1%), and the confidence gate needs about 52.2% there.
- **Change:** workers.env PROMOTION_GATE=progress; train.sh --promote-at 0.51 --max-games 4000 --extend-below 0.50 --extend-above 0.52 --extend-games 4000. A match plays 4000 games: 52% or more passes outright; 50-52% plays 4000 more on new boards and is judged at 51% on all 8000 (± 1.1%); under 50% fails. Checked in loop.py: the progress gate uses the same futility stop (after 1000 games it stops if the top of the 95% interval is under 50.5%, so about 47.4% or less); --min-games is read only by the SPRT gate, so it doesn't interfere. --min-score stays 0.
- **Guardrails unchanged:** anchor, benchmark, live-error guard (0.01). They are now the main protection against a false promotion.
- **Cost of a false promotion:** a true-50% net passes about 4% of the time (mostly via the 8000-game extension). It would replace round 73 on the bots and as the gate's reference, with no gain, or a loss against people the self-play match can't see. How we catch it: the four accounts' ranked win rate over the first 100 games on the new net, against round 73's 60% (232 games at 3 PM). Under about 50% after 100 games (two standard errors), roll back by restoring the best round in state.json (as with round 56).
- **Match time:** a 2000-game match takes about 13-25 min, so 4000 takes about 30-50 min and 8000 about 1-1.7 hours. Prefetched self-play runs alongside, so rounds get longer only when a match extends.
- **Metric:** a promotion; then live win rate over 100+ games.
- **Revert:** PROMOTION_GATE=confidence in workers.env; train.sh back to --extend-games 2000 without --max-games/--extend-below/--extend-above (git revert of the landing commit); safe_restart_training.sh.
- **First round:** the restart fires when round 96's match begins, so round 96 (trained from round 95's net) is the first match under E38.
- **LIVE (Oct 18, 3:36 PM):** landed 08e9eb3, workers.env PROMOTION_GATE=progress, colonist-train restarted; the loop resumed round 96's match as "progress gate, at most 4000 games" (it stood at 47.7% ± 2.8% after 1000 games, just above the futility stop). The log shows two resumes 43 s apart (3:36:02 and 3:36:45 PM): a second restart, harmless. Round 97 is the first round trained from the best net (E39).
- **CLOSED: KEPT (training, Oct 20, 11:30 PM Toronto).** Still the production gate (workers.env PROMOTION_GATE=progress; since E67 --extend-below 0.504). Three passes so far, all with anchor and benchmark held: round 150 (51.36% over 8000 vs r73, Oct 20, 8:28 AM), round 152 (51.01% over 8000, 9:59 AM), round 168 (51.12% over 8000 vs r152, 51.8% at 4000, Oct 20, 10:39 PM; anchor 64.2%, benchmark 95.2%, live MSE 0.557, by phase 0.888 / 0.481 / 0.385). The live check this entry set up has answered for r150/r152: the new nets went 25-28 on the accounts (11-19 vs 2100-2300), so live-play keeps round 73 on Dilly6283. A gate pass is not yet a live gain. Further gate changes go in new entries (E67).

## E39 — training: every round trains from the best net (owner, Oct 18, 3:02 PM; LIVE from round 97)
- **Why:** chained nets drift. Under --train-from latest, late live error rose about 0.003 a round (E31: 0.427 to 0.442 over rounds 89-94). Each round should be round 73 plus one clean step.
- **Change:** train.sh --train-from best (was latest). --selfplay-from latest stays: self-play is played by the newest trained net.
- **First round:** round 97 (its training starts after the restart; round 96 was already trained from round 95's net).
- **What this breaks:** E31's revert watch (rounds 95-97 at 2 epochs on the chain) has only rounds 95 and 96 clean: round 95's late error was 0.441 (round 94 0.442), so the climb has stopped for now. E35's phase comparison at rounds 99-101 against rounds 91-93 now mixes the spiral boards with E39's change; read it as both together.
- **Metric:** live error by phase (late at or under 0.43 again) and the gate score over 3 rounds.
- **Revert:** --train-from latest in train.sh, safe_restart_training.sh.
- **CLOSED: KEPT (training, Oct 20, 11:30 PM Toronto).** train.sh still runs --train-from best (now round 168). Late live error stopped climbing and fell: 0.441 (r95), 0.424-0.429 (r107-110), 0.385 (r168), under the 0.43 bar. On its own (r97-107, 6-round window) the gate scored 47.6-49.7% vs r73; the promotions came after E37's 18-round window was added on top.

## E40 — training: a deeper net, 5 message-passing rounds instead of 3 (PREPARED Oct 18, 3:20 PM; offline on the PC; the lead decides at 9 PM)
- **Why:** the owner likes a bigger net. E11b (Oct 16) trained three sizes on the same 3M positions at 12 epochs; all overfit, and the conclusion was that data, not size, was short. The size that did best there was deep (32,64,5): val value 0.400 against 0.411 control and 0.421 wide (48,96,3); policy 0.8418 against 0.8425 and 0.8428. It also costs less to run than wide (5/3 the message passing, about 1.7x, against about 2.25x the width). So E40 is the deep net, given 3-4x more distinct data than E11b had.
- **Design:** both arms from round 73. Deep = round 73 widened to 32,64,5 (graph.py widen(): two new rounds start near zero), control = round 73 as is. Data: rounds 80-97 (18 rounds, about 12M positions) plus the human folder x12. The PC loads at most 4M, so each arm trains in 3 legs of 4M distinct positions (by shard, seeds fixed and shared by both arms), 2 epochs a leg, lr 3e-4, ema 0.9997, each leg starting from the last. Only the size differs.
- **Time on the PC:** control about 3 x (50 s load + 2 x 185 s) = about 21 min; deep about 1.7x per epoch, about 3 x (50 + 2 x 315) = about 34 min. About 55 min both. In production a deep round would train in about 12 min instead of 8, and self-play and the bots would think about 1.7x slower per simulation (to be measured with bench before any live use).
- **When:** after E23's three arms finish, holding gpu.lock, starting in a match's first 2 minutes like E23 (the loop waits for gpu.lock).
- **Gate:** the E38 gate for each arm against round 73 (4000 games at 200 simulations, 52% passes, 50-52% extends to 8000 at 51%), deep vs control on the same boards, plus anchor and benchmark. Because the deep net is slower, adopt it only if it also wins at equal think time (1000+ games, 95% lower bound above 50%), and with the owner's OK: it changes the bots' speed.
- **Revert:** nothing deployed; offline only.
- **Moved to research (Oct 18, 8:40 PM Toronto).** Speed, measured (bench/selfplay.py, 2 games each, 200 simulations, coordinator CPU, deep = round 73 widened, so nearly the same play): round 73 827 us per simulation (network 442 us a call), deep 1049 us (network 669 us a call): **1.27x per simulation, 1.51x per network call**, not 1.7x. Equal think time is about 160 simulations for deep against 200 for round 73. Widened net: data/orchestrator/e40/deep_init.npz (graph.py widen, seed 0).
- **Blocker:** the PC stopped being the train host at round 104 and now plays self-play: 10 processes use about 7.8 of its 9 GB (MemAvailable 1.4 GB at 8:35 PM). A 4M-position leg needs about 5 GB, so E40 can't train there while self-play runs. Scripts ready: jobs/e40-run.sh (controller; MODE pc waits for 6 GB free on the PC, MODE window runs one leg per match window on another GPU host) and jobs/e40-arm.sh. Asked training to exclude the PC from self-play for about an hour, or to let research use gpu-train-1 for one leg (6 min at most) per match window.
- **LAUNCHED on gpu-train-1 (Oct 18, 8:31 PM Toronto), training's OK:** the PC stays in self-play; E40 uses production's GPU VM (owen@35.231.142.146, no extra cost, it runs anyway) in window mode: one leg per round, starting only in the first 2 min of a promotion match (about 22 min long), holding gpu.lock there; the loop's next training waits up to 20 min for it. A leg is about 3-6 min on the T4. Six legs (control-1, deep-1, ... deep-3) take about 6 rounds, about 3 hours, so both nets are ready about 11:30 PM. If a leg ever runs into the next round's training, stop it (scripts/vm/orchestrator/kill_job.sh e40 and remove gpu.lock on the VM). Controller pid 1320106, log jobs/e40.log; arms' log runs/e40/train.log on the VM; final nets copied to data/orchestrator/e40/{control,deep}/net.npz.
- **Matches (lead, Oct 18, 8:40 PM): not the self-play fleet.** Hosts: the PC (10 processes) and gpu-train-1 (8 cores, between trainings), 2 processes on the coordinator, all with the same board seeds. Order once both nets exist: control gate, deep gate, deep vs control (4000 each), equal time (deep at 160 against round 73 at 200, 1000+). Script: data/orchestrator/scratch/research/e40_gate.py; results in data/orchestrator/e40/results.txt. Note: the PC is also a self-play worker (10 processes), so matches there slow its self-play share while they run.
- **Rate test, Oct 18, 8:40-8:50 PM: stopped, the PC is not spare.** 200 games of round 73 against itself on the PC (10 processes) finished none in 10 min: the PC was also running round 108's self-play and round 107's promotion match (it is in the fleet's match hosts too). Load 28, 0.6 GB of 9.9 GB free, so stopped to protect production. Estimate from the fleet's own match (1000 games in 10 min, about 110 processes, about 65 s per game per process): PC + VM + 2 here, if free, about 0.3 games/s, 4000 games in about 3.7 h; the VM alone about 0.12 games/s, about 9 h. The lead's 1 game/s doesn't hold. Asked the lead for another host plan before the nets are ready (about 11:30 PM).

## E41 — training: position features stored as bytes in the training loader (lead, Oct 18, 3:30 PM; LANDED 5664a63, Oct 18, 3:54 PM)
- **Hypothesis:** most of the 570 dynamic features are counts over a fixed divisor, so storing the count in a byte halves the loader's memory with no change to training.
- **Change:** encode.DYNAMIC_BYTE_SCALE gives each feature's divisor (from the encoder's own code; 547 features). load_split stores k = x * s as uint8, and the 23 others (22 dice odds, the turn number, which reaches 300) as float16; Data.batch divides and rounds through float16, so it hands the net exactly the stored values. A value that isn't such a count prints a warning and is rounded.
- **Checks:** round 94 self-play (34,613 positions) and all 162,764 human positions (train + holdout) use exactly these divisors. The compact-data test still demands bit-identical batches. Same net (best), seed 7, 16,505 positions of round 94, 1 epoch on CPU, before vs after: train policy 0.938 value 0.378, val policy 0.855 value 0.372, accuracy 80.1%, aux 0.316, all identical. Batch build 30.5 vs 26.7 ms per 1,024 (CPU, noise level).
- **Result:** loaded bytes per position 1,240 -> 693. 18 rounds (about 12.1M positions) need about 8.4 GB instead of about 15. That is still tight on the PC (about 9 GB); comfortable on E42's 30 GB VM. The rest per position: packed legal moves, search policy shares, the per-game board, indexes.
- **Effect:** none until --train-max-positions is raised (E37 / E42 switch).
- **Revert:** git revert 5664a63.

## E47 — research: a weight average of the rounds trained from round 73 (lead, Oct 19, 12:05 AM; RUNNING from Oct 18, 11:58 PM)
- **Hypothesis:** rounds 97-110 each take a noisy step from round 73 (E39, different 4M/12M draws); averaging their weights cancels part of the noise and beats round 73 where each single round scores about 50%.
- **Nets** (data/orchestrator/e47/{A,B}/net.npz, built by scratch/research/e47_average.py; same keys and shapes, 49 float arrays averaged, nothing else differs): A = mean of rounds 108-110 (E37's 12M window); B = mean of the 9 rounds in 97-110 that scored ≥49% (101-104, 106-110).
- **Weight distances from round 73:** single rounds 97-107 about 2.5-2.65, rounds 108-110 about 5.1-5.2, A 4.99, B 3.01; round 108 to 109 is 2.22. So 108-110 moved mostly the same way and A is close to each of them.
- **Live value MSE** (data/human/holdout/value, 636 games, 24,961 positions; opening/middle/late): round 73 0.573 (0.885/0.503/0.414); round 110 0.581 (0.885/0.512/0.428); A 0.580 (0.886/0.511/0.426); B 0.580 (0.887/0.511/0.425). Guardrail (≤ round 73 + 0.01) holds for both.
- **Gate:** E38's, against round 73 at 200 simulations on the fleet (workers.env hosts, as the loop): 2,000 games, ≥52% passes, 50-52% plays 4,000 more (seed + 250,000) and passes at ≥51% on all, <50% fails. Seed 7,470,000,000 for both A and B (same boards). Job data/orchestrator/jobs/e47-run.sh (pid in e47.pid), results in data/orchestrator/e47/results.txt. A first, then B.
- **Guardrails for a net that passes:** anchor vs round 13 (2,000 games, loop's seed 3.7e9; must be ≥ 0.597 - 0.02, re-measure round 73 on spiral boards if narrow), benchmark AB:2 (400 games, loop's seed 3.9e9, vs best's recorded), live MSE above.
- **Cost:** fleet time only (each 2,000-game match slows a production round by about 10-20 minutes; accepted by the lead).
- **Revert:** nothing is live. An install would follow the round 56 procedure with its own revert written here first.
- **RESULT (Oct 19, 12:33 AM): FAILED, both.** A 48.60% ± 2.11% (972-1028, 2,000 games, 12:16 AM); B 48.45% ± 2.08% (969-1031, 2,000 games, 12:33 AM), same boards. Both under 50%, so the gate stops; no guardrails run, no install. Averaging did not beat the single rounds (rounds 101-110 scored 48.4-50.5% in their own gates). Reading: the rounds are not independent noisy steps around a better point; they all drift the same way (A is 4.99 from round 73 against 5.1-5.2 for each part), and that drift is what scores about 50%. Also seen: every net fine-tuned from round 73 has a higher live error than round 73 (0.573 → 0.580-0.582); a lead for the late-game target study.

- **RESULT (Oct 19, 1:46 AM): FAILED, the deeper net is not stronger.** Same boards (seed 7.4e9), 2,000 games each: control vs round 73 50.45% ± 2.16% (1009-991); deep vs round 73 48.25% ± 2.13% (965-1035); deep vs control 49.90% ± 2.09% (998-1002); deep at 160 simulations vs round 73 at 200 (equal think time, 253 vs 256 ms a move) 47.25% ± 2.08% (945-1055). Nets kept in data/orchestrator/e40/ for a later longer run if data grows.

- Oct 19, 3:45 AM: trained 1:07-1:32 AM. Validation after 4 epochs: policy 0.866, value 0.405 (epochs 2-4: 0.867/0.405, 0.867/0.405, 0.866/0.405; train policy 0.866 → 0.855, so the extra passes memorise). Live MSE (646 games): 0.579 (0.885/0.511/0.421), round 73 0.573 (0.886/0.504/0.409) on the same games. Gate started 3:42 AM on the fleet (jobs/e46g-run.sh, pid in e46g.pid, seed 7.46e9, results data/orchestrator/e46/results.txt).
