Thinking 255 vs 256 ms a move. Reading: one long, clean run over the full 18-round window is no stronger than a production round; with E39, E40, E47 and E48 it says the training procedure is not what holds the plateau: every way of fitting rounds 93-110 from round 73 lands at about 50%. No guardrails run, nothing installed.
- Oct 19, 3:59 AM: first 2,000 games 50.90% ± 2.14% (1018-982; 255 vs 256 ms a move), a near miss; 4,000 more games running (passes at 51% over 6,000).

## E48 — research: learning rate and EMA for the fine-tuning from round 73 (QUEUED Oct 19, 12:40 AM; after E46)
- **Hypothesis:** each production round (E39) is round 73 plus 2 epochs at lr 3e-4. That step is too big: every round lands at 49-50% against round 73 and its live late-game error rises (0.414 → 0.43). A smaller lr (1e-4, 3e-5) keeps what round 73 knows and keeps the new data's gain; or the EMA is what holds the rounds back (or helps).
- **Run:** 5 arms, all the same data, seed and length as one production round: round 73 init; rounds 93-110 plus human value x12 and policy_won x3; 2 epochs of a 12M sample; --max-positions 12M; seed 4800 (same shards, split and batches for every arm). Arms: lr3e-4-ema (production's settings, the control), lr1e-4-ema, lr3e-5-ema (EMA 0.9997), lr3e-4-raw, lr1e-4-raw (no EMA). Warm-up left out: train.py has no warm-up flag; added only if the lr arms show a signal. On the GPU VM by data/orchestrator/jobs/e48-run.sh (log jobs/e48.log), one arm (about 12 minutes) in a round's match window, or a second in the same match if it is under 15 minutes old. gpu.lock held. Nets to data/orchestrator/e48/<arm>/.
- **Metric:** validation policy and value loss (same split for all arms); live error on people's games (live_mse.py, 636 games), overall and late, against round 73's 0.573 / 0.414. Then the gate against round 73 for the best two arms by these: 2,000 games at 200 simulations, seed 7.48e9; 52% passes, 50-52% plays 4,000 more at 51%. A lower lr that stays at 49-50% but keeps the late error near 0.414 is still a finding for training.
- **Games until a verdict:** 2,000-6,000 per gated arm (2 arms), about 18 min each on the fleet; only when the lead says the fleet has room.
- **Cost:** none beyond the VM already running; about 1 hour of VM GPU in 5 match windows. **Revert:** nothing is live; stop with kill_job.sh e48 --yes and remove gpu.lock on the VM if an arm was cut off.
- Oct 19, 3:45 AM (RUNNING since 1:58 AM): 4 of 5 arms done, lr1e-4-raw training since 3:29 AM. Last-epoch validation policy / value: lr3e-4-ema 0.8667 / 0.4029; lr1e-4-ema 0.8685 / 0.4033; lr3e-5-ema 0.8705 / 0.4061; lr3e-4-raw 0.8661 / 0.4052. Live MSE (646 games; round 73 0.573, late 0.409): lr3e-4-ema 0.579 (late 0.423), lr1e-4-ema 0.580 (0.422), lr3e-5-ema 0.580 (0.421), lr3e-4-raw 0.580 (0.424). Reading so far: smaller lr is a little worse on validation and does not keep the live error at round 73's; even lr 3e-5 for 2 epochs moves the live error from 0.573 to 0.580. So the step size is not why rounds lose round 73's live accuracy; the data is (task 3). Production's settings (lr3e-4-ema) are best on validation value. Differences are 0.001-0.003, near the noise of one seed.
- Oct 19, 3:51 AM DONE-FAILED (no lever): lr1e-4-raw 0.8676 / 0.4021 (best value of the five, by 0.0008), live 0.580 (late 0.423), 648 games. All five arms: live 0.579-0.580, late 0.421-0.424, against round 73's 0.573 / 0.409. Neither a smaller lr (down to 3e-5) nor dropping the EMA keeps round 73's live accuracy; validation spreads 0.002-0.004 (one seed). No gate games spent: with no offline signal, a gate would only re-measure E46/production's 49-50%. Warm-up not built (no lr signal to justify it). Production's lr 3e-4 / EMA 0.9997 stays. Next: task 3, the data (late-game value on people's games).

## E49 — research: why the late game against people gets worse after round 73 (DIAGNOSTIC, started Oct 19, 3:51 AM; no GPU, no games)
- **Question:** E16 (value target mix), E23 (weight on people's games) and E48 (lr / EMA) all fail to keep round 73's live late error (0.409); every net trained after it lands at 0.42-0.43. Is round 73 a lucky outlier, or does the newer self-play data drift away from how people play late?
- **Step 1 (running):** live_mse.py on production nets 60, 66, 70, 72, 73, 74, 76, 80, 85, 90, 97, 100, 105, 110, 115 (648 holdout games). Job e49d (jobs/e49d-run.sh, log jobs/e49d.log). Reading: if 73 sits alone below its neighbours, it is noise in one round and the "lost accuracy" is not a real target; if late error climbs steadily with round, the data drifts and step 2 compares the late-game positions of rounds 55-72 with 93-110 (lead size at turn 75+, how often a lead converts in self-play vs in people's games).
- **Then a proposal** (one of: a turns-to-the-end auxiliary target; value on people's games at turn 60+ only; a late-game value shrink) with a measurable design, before anything is built. **Cost:** CPU only on the coordinator. **Revert:** nothing deployed.
- **RESULT (DONE Oct 19, 4:00 AM): the late live error is not a lever worth a training change.** 648 holdout games, 25 440 positions (6 575 at turn 60+); differences between nets are paired (same positions).
  - **Step 1, late MSE by round:** 60 0.412, 66 0.420, 70 0.409, 72 0.406, 73 0.408, 74 0.406, 76 0.404, 80 0.405, 85 0.412, 90 0.421, 97 0.422, 100 0.416, 105 0.422, 110 0.423, 115 0.419. Round 73 is not an outlier: rounds 70-80 form a 0.404-0.409 block, rounds 85+ sit at 0.412-0.423, round 66 at 0.420. Overall MSE moves only 0.570-0.581.
  - **Step 2, self-play data does not drift** (scratch/research/late_cal.py, 25 shards a round, about 1 350-1 600 games): at turns 75-90 a search value ≥ 0.5 claims +0.89-0.90 and the game result is +0.84-0.90 in every round from 58 to 112, no trend; bias +0.00 to +0.04; median last turn 80-82; 63-69% of games reach turn 75. In self-play a late lead converts as the net says; against people it converts less (Oct 17 diagnostic: +0.86 claimed, +0.67 realised at turns 75-90).
  - **Step 3, scale vs ranking** (scratch/research/late_shrink.py, turns 60+): the best single shrink v → c·v is c 0.96-0.97 and changes the MSE by at most 0.001 (r73 0.408 → 0.408; turns 75-90 0.471 → 0.468). The ranking of positions (AUC for the bot's win) is the same for every net: r73 0.9408, r76 0.9419, r97 0.9401, r105 0.9402, E46 0.9403. So the nets' late errors differ in a few hard positions, not in a general over-confidence a shrink or a target mix can remove; and a uniform rescale would not change which move the search picks anyway.
  - **Reading:** round 73 (late 0.408) and round 66 (0.420) win the same share live (59.0% vs 59.2%, ~350 games each), so a 0.01 late-MSE gap has no visible Elo. Closing task 3's calibration route: no value shrink, no target-mix rerun. The remaining gap is a domain gap (people come back from leads that self-play opponents don't); the candidate that addresses it is data that plays like people late (more human-opponent self-play or human-seat starts past turn 60), not a loss change.

## E50 — research: late starts from people's games with the human seat in the person's chair (PROPOSED Oct 19, 4:10 AM; NOT BUILT; recommendation: don't build yet)
- **Why:** E49's reading: the late gap is that people come back from leads, which self-play never shows. Already tried and null: E13 (human seat in 15% of games, from move 1) and E27 (the same seat holding knights like people). Both start from an empty board, so the seat spends its game in early positions and the net beats it 78-88%; few of its games reach a close late position. starts.py cuts only at turns 16-60, and in a human start both sides are then played by the net, so no person-like play follows the cut.
- **Change (would be):** a second starts file cut at turns 60-100 (`starts.py --min-turn 60 --max-turn 100`, a new output path); in those games the holddev human seat (E27's model) takes the person's chair from the cut on, the net the bot's. Code: selfplay.py would need a flag tying the human seat to the person's colour in a human start (about 30 lines plus a test).
- **Offline test:** one round's worth of these games only (about 1,500 games at training sims, on the PC's CPU when its self-play workers are paused, or a spot VM within the cap), added to rounds 93-110 at 1x, fine-tuned from round 73 with production's settings (2 epochs, lr 3e-4, EMA 0.9997, seed shared with a no-late-starts control).
- **Metric / verdict:** (1) late live MSE paired vs the control on 648 holdout games, bar -0.010 (E49's spread across rounds); (2) l300 army + dev slice paired vs control ≤ -0.03; (3) then the E38 gate vs round 73 (2,000 games, 52% passes). Fails if (1) misses its bar.
- **Why not build yet:** E49 found that a 0.01 late error gap has no visible Elo (round 66 vs round 73 live: 59.2% vs 59.0%, about 350 games each). Even a full success on (1) would not show live. The project's question is where the missing strength is; the lead's opponent-rating task to live-loss answers that first. Cost if built: about 1.5 hours of code and tests, one round of CPU self-play, one 25-minute GPU run, one 18-minute gate.
- **Revert:** nothing deployed.

## E51 — training: self-play full searches at 800 simulations instead of 400 (lead, Oct 19, 9:00 AM Toronto; RUNNING)
- **Started (training, Oct 19, 8:45 AM):** SELFPLAY_SIMULATIONS=800 in workers.env; safe_restart_training.sh queued, fires once round 122's match begins and round 123's early self-play (400 sims, already under way) is done. Round 124's self-play is the first at 800 sims, so round 124 is the first 800-sim round and the verdict pools rounds 131-136 (8-13 after it). Baseline self-play time: 5,000 games in about 14 min.
- **Applied (training, Oct 19, 9:51 AM):** the restart fired at round 123's match; the loop now runs --simulations 800. Round 124's early self-play had already finished at 400 (9:42 AM, 5,000 games), so **round 125 is the first 800-sim round and the verdict pools rounds 133-138** (8-13 after it), not 131-136.
- **Hypothesis:** the loop is at a fixed point: every recipe trained on round 73's self-play data scores about 50% against round 73 (E39, E40, E46, E47, E48; rounds 111-121 49.9% over 68,000 games). In an AlphaZero-style loop the gain per round is how much the search improves on the net; E18 put a doubling of simulations at 1-2 points here. Better targets from a longer full search should move the trained net above round 73.
- **Change:** SELFPLAY_SIMULATIONS=800 in workers.env (full-rate 0.25 and fast 24 unchanged), applied by safe_restart_training.sh at a safe point. Subsystem: self-play/training (the one experiment there; E37 stays as the baseline setting).
- **Cost:** self-play per game about 1.85x; the fleet now finishes 5,000 games in about 14 min of a 33-min round, so no wall-clock cost expected. Trigger to revert early: self-play slower than training plus match by more than 10 min for 3 rounds.
- **Metric:** gate score vs round 73 pooled over rounds 8-13 after the first 800-sim round (a third to two thirds of the 18-round window), against 49.9% ± 0.2 for rounds 111-121. Above 51% keeps it (a promotion expected as the window fills); 50% or under reverts.
- **Revert:** SELFPLAY_SIMULATIONS=400 and a safe restart.
- Research measures alongside: round 73 at 400 and 800 simulations vs the raw net and vs 24 simulations (fleet, 1,000 games each, in match windows), and the KL between the 400-simulation improved policy and the prior on self-play positions.
- **Research's measurement (Oct 19, 9:20 AM): the search does improve on the net, so E51 has something to carry.** Fleet, 1,000 games each, same boards (seed 7.51e9), all round 73, players as in the gate (Z, Gumbel noise on): **400 sims vs raw policy 58.9% ± 2.8%** (589-411; 499 vs 2 ms a move); **800 vs 400 54.1% ± 3.1%** (541-459; 1184 vs 580 ms); 24 vs raw 44.0% ± 2.9% (440-560: at 24 sims with the gate's exploration noise the search is worse than the raw top move; self-play's fast moves run without noise, so this doesn't describe them). 400 clears the lead's 55% bar. 800 vs 400 is more than E18's 1-2 points at 1200-4800, as expected from diminishing returns.
- **Targets vs prior.** (a) Round 122's shards (played by round 73 at 400, 10 shards, 22,663 full-search non-setup positions; scratch/research/e51_kl.py shards): KL(target ‖ round 73's prior) 0.410 ± 0.009 nats (median 0.123), the target's top move differs from the prior's in 23.6%; by phase early 0.505 / mid 0.349 / late 0.464 (late top-1 differs 27.9%); |search value − net value| 0.088. (b) Paired searches on the same positions (8 games, 314 positions, a quarter of non-setup decisions; e51_kl.py play): 400 vs prior KL 0.287 ± 0.063 (top-1 differs 18.8%); 800 vs prior 0.353 ± 0.079 (18.2%); **two 400 searches of the same position differ by KL 0.379 (median 0.001), top-1 differs 10.5%**; 800 vs 400 0.558 (median 0.009), top-1 differs 16.6%.
- **Reading.** The search is worth about 9 points over the raw net and 800 adds about 4 more, yet a net trained on these targets ends up where it began (round 73's prior still sits KL 0.41 from its own targets). Two things limit what training absorbs: the targets are noisy (two 400 searches disagree on the top move 1 time in 10, with a heavy tail of near one-hot flips), and the net may lack the capacity to follow them (E53 tests width). 800 sims cut neither by much: its target is only a little further from the prior than 400's (0.35 vs 0.29) and differs from 400's mostly within the noise. Expect a small E51 effect, if any; its gate is the test.
- **Self-play time (efficiency, Oct 19, 12:20 PM):** start of the early self-play to its last log, 5,000 games. At 400: rounds 122, 123, 124 took 12.4, 11.5, 12.4 min. At 800: rounds 125, 126, 127 took 26.5, 30.1, 31.3 min (about 2.4x; one spot VM was down during 125 and 127). Training plus match alongside: round 124 24.4 min (match stopped at 1,000 games), round 125 45.0 min (match ran on), round 126 over 40 min (still in its match at 12:18 PM). So self-play now sets the pace when a match stops at 1,000 games (round 125 waited 2.7 min), and costs nothing when it runs on. The revert trigger (10+ min slower for 3 rounds) is not near. 2.4x rather than 1.85x: the full searches now take most of a game's time.
- **Rounds so far (training, Oct 19, 2:45 PM):** gates vs round 73: r125 49.70/4000, r126 50.96/8000, r127 49.38/4000, r128 50.72/8000 (near miss, no action), r129 49.5 after 1,000 (running). First reading at 9 PM pools rounds 125-132; the verdict stays rounds 133-138. **Self-play-time log note:** while selfplay-spot-11 and -12 (owen@10.128.0.8, .9) are out of workers.env for E54 (up to 5 h, from research's request), self-play is about 8% slower; those rounds are not counted toward the 10-min revert trigger. Window written here when it starts and ends.
- **E54 loan started (training, Oct 19, 3:11 PM):** both hosts out of workers.env (hosts and capacities lines; read every round, no restart). Round 131's self-play began at 3:05 PM with them, so **round 132's self-play is the first without them**; rounds 132 until their return don't count toward the revert trigger. r129 49.79/8000 (kept r73); live MSE 0.573 (0.890/0.503/0.407).
- **E54 loan ended (training, Oct 19, 4:56 PM):** research stopped E54 and handed both hosts back (5:10 PM message). Both are back in workers.env (hosts `:4`, capacities 33). Round 133's self-play (started 4:39 PM) runs without them; their first work is round 132's match or round 134's self-play. Rounds 132-133 don't count toward the revert trigger; the E51 verdict rounds 134-138 have the full fleet, 133 is short by two hosts.

## E52 — efficiency: restart a preempted spot VM as soon as a round loses it (lead, Oct 19, 9:00 AM Toronto; live Oct 19, 9:51 AM; DONE, KEPT Oct 20, 8:50 AM)
- **Verdict (Oct 20, 8:50 AM): kept; target missed.** gcloud operations from Oct 19, 9:51 AM to Oct 20, 8:45 AM (no preemption after 6:54 AM, so this is the full day in practice): 29 preemptions, each paired with the same VM's next start: **median 6.1 min down** (before about 20; the 24 h before E52: 18 preemptions, mean 20.4), mean 8.4, 12 of 29 under 5 min. Total down 243 min against 367 min the day before, despite 29 preemptions instead of 18. train.log has 28 "is down: starting" lines. Slow ones (6 of 29 at 14-26 min) fit the known limit: a VM lost while the round trains is only seen at the match's preparation. Follow-up if wanted: a check during training. Script: scratch/efficiency/e52_downtime.py (deleted after use; pairs preempted with the next start per VM).
- **Early reading (Oct 19, 12:20 PM):** 5 preemptions since 9:51 AM (spot-7, spot-4 three times, spot-8), back in 15, 15, 13, 8 and 6 min: median 13 (was about 20). Verdict at 24 h, Oct 20, 9:51 AM.
- **Add-on (Oct 19, 12:30 PM, lead's decision 12:10 PM):** gcp_start_spot.sh moves the GPU VM to us-east1-b or -d when us-east1-c has no capacity (machine image, same name and static IP, old VM deleted once the new one runs). Log: runs/gcp_gpu_start.log. Untested on a real stockout (dry run only).
- **Problem:** 16 spot preemptions in the 24 h to Oct 19, 7:45 AM (spot-9 6, spot-10 4, spot-6 4, spot-11 1, spot-7 1), down 3 to 46 min each, median about 20, because only the next round's start (gcp_start_spot.sh in --before-round) restarted them. About 5 VM-hours a day lost.
- **Change:** distributed.py: when a host can't be prepared (self-play or match) or drops out with games unfinished, it starts gcp_start_spot.sh in the background, at most once per host in 10 min; it never blocks the round. train.sh turns it on (COLONIST_RESTART_SCRIPT); off elsewhere (tests, research matches). No zone moves, no new VMs, no cost.
- **Metric:** median downtime per preemption, from the preempted to the matching start operations (gcloud compute operations list), 24 h after it is live: from about 20 min to under 5. train.log shows "is down: starting stopped spot VMs" lines.
- **Limit:** a VM preempted while the round trains is only noticed at the match's preparation (training about 14 min).
- **Revert:** remove the export line in train.sh and a safe restart.

## E53 — research: a net twice as wide (64,128,3), trained from scratch (lead, Oct 19, 9:00 AM; PLANNED Oct 19, 9:10 AM; offline, GPU VM between rounds)
- **Why:** the last untried PLAN.md fallback ("a bigger net"). Every recipe that starts from round 73 lands at 50% against it (E39, E40, E46, E47, E48): the loop sits at a fixed point. A fresh start at twice the width leaves that point. E51's measurement (below in E51) shows round 73 does not learn its own search's targets: after training on them, its prior still differs by KL 0.41 nats and picks a different top move 24% of the time, while its search at 400 beats the raw net 58.9%. A wider net may close more of that gap.
- **Net:** graph net 64,128,3 (round 73 is 32,64,3; about 3.4x the parameters), --arch graph --graph 64,128,3, random start. Speed (bench/selfplay.py, 2 games, 200 simulations, coordinator under load 14, so rough): network call 1.5-2.2x round 73's, about 1.2-1.6x per simulation. To re-measure on a quiet host before any live use.
- **Data:** production's at the start (the 18-round window ending at the latest round, human value folder x12 and policy_won x3, as train.sh builds it); frozen for the whole run so the legs see the same data.
- **Recipe:** legs of 1 epoch each on a 12M-position load (--max-positions 12M, about 4 min to load on the VM, about 8-11 min per wide epoch), each leg starting from the last (--init), lr 1e-3 for legs 1-4 then 3e-4 for legs 5-8 (train.py's cosine runs within each leg), EMA 0.9997, seeds 5300+leg. 8 legs, about 8 epochs over 12M, so about 96M samples (production's from-scratch round 1 nets had far fewer).
- **Where and when:** the GPU VM (owen@35.231.142.146), one leg per round, started only after a round's 'promotion match' line and holding gpu.lock; a leg must end within about 15 min, so the first leg is timed and the load is cut to 8M if it runs over. About 8 rounds, 5-6 hours. Nets to data/orchestrator/e53/leg-N/net.npz.
- **Checkpoints:** after legs 4 and 8, validation policy/value loss next to round 122's (0.859/0.409), and live MSE (live_mse.py) next to round 73's 0.573.
- **Metric:** after leg 8, the E38 gate against round 73 (200 simulations each, 2000 games, 52% passes, 50-52% plays 4000 more and passes at 51%). Then equal think time (wide at the simulations that match round 73's 200 in ms per move, 1000+ games, lower bound above 50%). Adoption is the owner's call: it slows the bots.
- **Control (only if the wide net fails):** the same recipe at 32,64,3 from scratch, to tell whether width or the fresh start is to blame.
- **Cost:** no money (the VM runs anyway); about 1.5 h of VM GPU time in 8 windows. **Revert:** nothing is live; stop with kill_job.sh e53 --yes and remove gpu.lock on the VM.
- **Started Oct 19, 9:25 AM.** The load is 10M positions, not 12M (production's 2 epochs of the small net take about 10 min plus 4 min loading; a wide epoch is 1.5-2.2x, so 12M would run past a 15-min gate). Data frozen to data/orchestrator/e53/frozen (rounds 105-122, the human folders as of 9:25 AM) and copied to runs/e53/data on the VM. The VM was preempted when this started (the loop restarts it); legs wait for it. Controller jobs/e53-run.sh, leg jobs/e53-leg.sh, log jobs/e53.log.
- **Oct 19, 1:25 PM: checkpoint after leg 4 (on track, no verdict).** Legs 1-5 done (12:05 PM leg 4, 1:03 PM leg 5), each epoch about 410 s on 9.5M positions. Validation policy / value by leg: 1.000/0.430, 0.936/0.423, 0.915/0.411, 0.900/0.404, 0.892/0.406 (leg 5, first at lr 3e-4); round 122 on its own window 0.859/0.409. So value is already level with production, policy still 0.03 behind. Live value MSE (data/human/holdout/value, 677 games, 26,587 positions, same set for both): leg 4 0.577 (opening 0.899, middle 0.508, late 0.403) against round 73's 0.567 (0.888 / 0.496 / 0.399); production rounds sit at 0.576-0.578, so the wide net from scratch is where a production round is after 4 epochs. Legs 6-8 next (about 3 more rounds), then the gate.
- **Oct 19, 1:45 PM: think time measured (coordinator, load 12 on 8 cores, so rough).** Leg-4 net against round 73, local evaluate, 8 games each, 2 workers, same boards: at 200 simulations each, 632 vs 406 ms a move (1.56x); wide at 128 vs round 73 at 200, 438 vs 473 ms. So equal think time is about 140 simulations for the wide net against 200 for round 73. The equal-time match after leg 8: Z:<leg-8>:140 vs Z:round_073:200, 1000+ games, recheck the ms per move in its output.

## E54 — research: policy-gradient training without search, against a pool of past nets (PROPOSED Oct 19, 9:45 AM; a plan, not built; the lead decides)
- **Why:** owner's direction (Oct 19, 9:25 AM): we are not bound to the AlphaZero loop. E51's numbers say the teacher is barely ahead of the student and noisy (400 sims beat the raw net 58.9% ± 2.8; two 400-searches pick different top moves 10.5% of the time), which is the fixed point. Policy gradient learns from game outcomes instead of from round 73's own search, so it is not tied to that point. And it is cheap: measured Oct 19, 9:30 AM, round 73 raw vs raw (Z:0, which samples from the prior) plays **100 games in 19 s on one loaded core, 0.19 s a game** (0.9 ms a move). A production self-play game costs about 11 s of a core (E13's probe), so **about 60x the games per core**. E11b said the net is data-limited.
- **Caution from today's numbers:** the raw net loses 41-59 to its own 400-sim search, so search is worth about 9 points here (E18's 1-2 points was for doubling simulations, not for search itself). The aim is a better net to put inside the same search, not dropping search. Both are measured.
- **Code (new, offline, nothing in production changes):**
  1. `catanatron/colonist/pg.py` (new): the actor. Plays many games at once with batched raw-net calls (reusing selfplay.py's game loop and net batching, no search), samples moves from the prior at temperature 1, and records for the learner's seat: features, legal mask, move, the move's log-probability, the value head's output, and the final result. Opponents drawn per game from a pool: 50% the current learner (both seats recorded), 50% one of round 73, 66, 13 and the human-style seat (models/human/net.npz, as in E13), one seat recorded. Shards in the same npz layout as self-play plus the logp and value columns.
  2. `train.py --pg` (new mode, off by default): the PPO loss. Advantage from GAE on the value head (lambda 0.95, reward +1/-1 at the end, no discount), which uses the value head as the baseline and cuts the dice noise in the reward; clipped ratio (0.2); value MSE to the lambda-returns; entropy bonus 0.01; and a KL penalty to round 73's prior (beta 0.02 to start) so the prior stays usable inside search. Old nets keep loading: same architecture, same npz.
  3. `scratch/research/e54_loop.py`: actor-learner loop on frozen settings. One iteration = 20,000 games (about 2.4M learner rows) then 1 epoch; every 10 iterations a checkpoint goes to data/orchestrator/e54/iter-N/net.npz. No loop.py changes.
- **Where:** learner on the PC's GPU (gpu.lock; PPO epochs on 2-3M rows take 1-2 min). Actors need about 16 cores: the PC's self-play workers stay as configured, so either a fleet share between rounds (the lead's call; E51 now uses more of the round) or one 16-vCPU spot VM within the cap (efficiency to price it). At 0.19 s a game, 16 cores play 20,000 games in about 4 min: about 10 iterations, 200,000 games an hour, against production's about 6,400.
- **Days to a first gate match:** about 2. Day 1: build pg.py and --pg with tests, smoke run (2 iterations). Day 2: 50 iterations (1M games, 5-8 h), then the gate.
- **Metric and verdict:** (1) early signal: raw vs raw against round 73 (Z:0 each, 1,000 games, ±3%) every 10 iterations; if under 52% after 30 iterations (600,000 games), stop. (2) The E38 gate against round 73 at 200 simulations each (4,000 games, 52% passes; 50-52% plays to 8,000 and passes at 51%). (3) If it passes: 300 live games on one account against that account's recent record (the owner's call; 300 games are ±5.6%, so only a large change shows).
- **Biggest risk:** a policy that gets better raw but sharper, which makes the search worse (the search wants a calibrated prior). The KL to round 73 and gating with search guard against it. Second risk: 1v1 dice noise needs more games than we think; the early signal at 600,000 games answers that cheaply.
- **Cost:** no money if the fleet share is used; else one spot VM for about a day. About 8 h of the PC's GPU over 2 days.
- **Oct 19, 9:45 AM: code step 1 built (offline; nothing runs).** `catanatron/colonist/pg.py` plays games without search, batched per net, and writes shards with action, logp, vpred, adv and ret (GAE lambda 0.95 along each seat's moves, reward z at the end). Smoke: round 73 learner, pool round 66 + human seat, 100 games in 20.5 s on the loaded coordinator (0.205 s a game; batching doesn't help, the game engine is the cost), 15,810 rows; z mean +0.09 (the learner's share 54.7% over 100 games, noise), value-head MSE to z 0.48, mean logp -0.86; train.py's load_split reads the shard. Tests in tests/colonist/test_pg.py. Next: step 2, train.py --pg.
- **Oct 19, 10:10 AM: code step 2 built and landed (bc7effe; offline, off by default).** `train.py --pg` trains with PPO on pg.py's shards: clipped ratio 0.2 on per-batch normalised advantages, value head to the GAE return, entropy 0.01, KL to a frozen prior (`--pg-prior`, default `--init`; `--pg-kl 0.02`). A test checks the ratio is exactly 1 and the KL 0 for the net that played the games, so the logp recorded in play and the training forward pass agree. Smoke: round 73 self-play, 200 games (38,585 rows, 0.136 s a game), 2 epochs at lr 3e-5 on the coordinator's CPU, 30 s an epoch: KL to round 73 0.0017, 1.3% of rows clipped, value MSE to the return 0.083 on validation. Next: step 3, scratch/research/e54_loop.py (needs cores; the lead's call).
- **Oct 19, 1:25 PM: step 3 built and tested (offline).** `scratch/research/e54_loop.py OUT --iters N --games G --workers W --device D`: W pg.py processes play G games (half self-play, half against round 73, 66, 13 or the people model), 1 epoch of train.py --pg from the current learner with KL to round 73, next net in OUT/iter-N/net.npz; raw-vs-raw against round 73 every 10 iterations into OUT/eval.txt; restartable; shards deleted once used. Smoke on the coordinator CPU (data/orchestrator/e54/smoke): 2 iterations of 400 games on 2 cores; play **0.10 s a game per core** (faster than the 0.19-0.2 s measured this morning on a busier box), about 135 rows a game; learning 65-70 s an epoch on CPU for 55,000 rows; KL to round 73 0.0013 then 0.0028, 0.9% of rows clipped. Raw vs raw against round 73 after 2 iterations: 43-57 over 100 games (±9.7, noise, as expected from 800 games of learning). **Sizing for the real run:** an iteration of 20,000 games is about 2.7M rows; play takes about 2,000 core-seconds (2 min on 16 cores), learning about 1 min on the PC's GPU but about 55 min on CPU, so the learner must be on a GPU. 30 iterations (600,000 games, the early-signal stop) is about 2 h with 16 cores and the PC's GPU. Waiting for the lead: where the 16 cores come from (the PC's own cores with its self-play workers paused for 2 h, a fleet share, or a 16-vCPU spot VM via efficiency).

## E55 — research: train against a people-like opponent (PROPOSED Oct 19, 9:45 AM; CLOSED Oct 20, 11:40 PM: step 1 done, step 2 never run)
- **Why:** the live gap is against people: late value error 0.43 live vs 0.34 in self-play, and the bots win 48% against 2300+ players. **What already exists:** self-play has had a human-style seat in 15% of games since round 74 (E13; with people's habit of holding knights since round 79, E27), and the bridge can mix a people model into the search (E2). Both were null: no change in late error, gate or live. The seat model is round 66 fine-tuned on people's moves (66.5% top-1 on 16,448 held-out moves), it loses 78-88% of its games against the net, and it plays its cards like the net. So the new part of B is a better people model and a stronger way to learn from it, not the seat itself.
- **Code:**
  1. A behaviour-cloned model trained on people's moves only (human_dataset.py's opponent shards, 4,687 games in data/human/index.jsonl), from random weights instead of from the net, plus a held-out check: top-1 and cross-entropy against today's 66.5% / 0.915, and the share of action cards held at turn 60+ (people 1.37 [1.29, 1.46]). opponent_model.py already trains it; only the start and settings change (no new code, live-loss's data).
  2. Training against it with E54's machinery: the pool's human slot raised from about 12% to 50% of games, the learner on outcomes, KL to round 73. No change to loop.py; no new self-play code (selfplay.py --human-opponent already plays the seat).
- **Where:** model training on the PC's GPU (about 1 h); the rest as E54.
- **Days to a first gate match:** about 1 after E54's machinery works (3 if built alone, by self-play at a higher human-seat rate, which E13 already tried at 15%).
- **Metric and verdict:** (1) the people model must predict people better than today's (cross-entropy below 0.90 on the holdout, ±0.01), else stop: without a better model, B repeats E13. (2) Late value error on the l300 late set (people's games), paired against round 73: -0.03 or better is a signal. (3) Gate against round 73: at least 49% (it may trade self-play strength for strength against people). (4) 300 live games, as E54.
- **Biggest risk:** the model isn't people. 4,687 games are thin; a model trained only on them may be weaker than the fine-tuned one, and a net that learns to beat a model's quirks gains nothing live (E14 and E23 added people's games to training and nothing showed live).
- **Cost:** no money; 1 h of PC GPU plus E54's.
- **Oct 19, 10:05 AM: the exact-hand export changes step 1 (owner's decision, 9:45 AM).** The people model trains on train/opponent_exact and is scored on holdout/opponent_exact. Why it matters: today's opponent shards keep only the person's turns with **no** unplayed development card (human_dataset.py), so the people model has never seen a turn where it held a knight. It cannot have learned when people hold or play one; that is why the seat played its cards like the net and needed E27's hand-set -3 bias. The kept turns also lean early (late in the game people usually hold a card), which is where the live gap is. Changes:
  1. Holdout: today's model is re-scored on holdout/opponent_exact (its 0.915 / 66.5% is on the card-free slice and doesn't compare). Pass: cross-entropy 0.01+ below today's on the same holdout, and on the dev-card turns alone.
  2. The holddev patch: expected to go away, decided by measurement, not assumed. With bias 0, the new seat's action cards held at turn 60+ (E27's check: r66 at 100 sims, 48 games) must fall in or near people's 1.37 [1.29, 1.46]. In: drop the patch. Out: keep a re-calibrated bias.
  3. Start: two arms, 1 h each on the PC: from random weights and from r66 (today's start), same data. The better one on the holdout goes on.
  4. Order: the people model no longer waits for E54. It is cheap and independent; train it as soon as the export lands. If it passes, it can replace the 15% seat in production (training's call), and it is a better pool member for E54. Step 2 (50% of games against it) still needs E54.
  - **Bias to check in the export:** a hand is exact only if later plays and the end-of-game reveal account for every card. Unless colonist reveals all cards at the end, a knight held to the end and never played can't be backfilled, so games where people hoard may drop out, the very behaviour we want. Asked live-loss for the share of the person's turns kept and the held-card count at turn 60+ in kept vs all games.
  - Option A (E54) is unaffected.
- **Oct 19, 10:20 AM: step 1 RUNNING** (coordinator CPU, nice, 3 threads, not the PC: 183k moves is about 1 min an epoch; jobs/e55-run.sh, pid in jobs/e55.pid, log jobs/e55.log, nets in data/orchestrator/e55/). Arm r73: opponent_model train from round 73, 10 epochs (what E56's refresh will do). Arm random: graph 32,64,3 from random weights, lr 1e-3, 20 epochs, same other settings. Baseline on holdout/opponent_exact (665 games, 31,746 moves; scratch/research/e55_score.py): today's models/human/net.npz top-1 67.1%, CE 0.909; on the 7,330 dev-playable decisions (a play-card move legal) 70.0% / 0.858, its top move plays a card 22.4% vs the person 20.8%. r73 itself 61.6% / 1.112 (dev-playable 0.976). Pass: CE 0.899 or lower overall and 0.848 or lower on dev-playable.
- **Oct 19, 11:15 AM: arm r73 PASSES step 1.** data/orchestrator/e55/r73/net.npz (best epoch of 10, train-split val policy 0.838). On holdout/opponent_exact (668 games, 31,854 moves; the holdout had grown 3 games since the baseline, re-scored together): today's 67.1% / CE 0.909, new 70.1% / 0.835 (-0.074). Dev-playable decisions (7,361): today's 70.1% / 0.856, new 79.3% / 0.614 (-0.242); its top move plays a card 19.5% vs the person 20.8% (today's 22.4%). Rough 95% interval on the paired CE gap, clustered by game: about ±0.01, so far outside noise. Arm random: at epoch 5 val 0.973 against arm r73's 0.838, still running but starved (the coordinator is busy with a nice-10 match; one epoch took 1,068 s); it won't catch up, and its score lands in jobs/e55.log. This is the model E56's refresh will make (round 73 start, opponent_exact), so training's refresh will replace models/human/net.npz on its first run. Next: the holddev check with this model as the seat, bias 0 (cards held at turn 60+ against people's 1.37 [1.29, 1.46]).
- **Oct 19, 1:20 PM: holddev check: the new model holds FEWER cards late, not more. The patch stays.** Both people models as the seat at dev_play_bias 0, against round 73 at 100 simulations, 48 games each on the same seeds (scratch/research/holddev_e55.py, jobs/hd55.log): action cards held at turn 60+, today's model 0.75 [0.54, 0.99] (net won 32/48), new r73-arm model **0.40 [0.31, 0.48]** (net won 34/48); people 1.37 [1.29, 1.46]. So better move prediction on the holdout (79% vs 70% on card decisions) does not carry over to holding cards in play. Likely cause, the bias noted at 10:05 AM: a hand is "exact" only when later plays or the end reveal account for every card, so turns of people who sat on a knight to the end are under-represented in opponent_exact. Consequence for E56: the refresh's new models/human/net.npz is fine as a predictor, but the self-play seat keeps E27's holddev file and bias; a seat built on the new model would need a stronger bias than -3 (to be calibrated with this same check if training wants it). Arm random finished far behind (val 0.900 at epoch 17 vs 0.838). E55 step 1 closed: model passes as a predictor, fails as a hoarder.
- **Oct 20, 11:40 PM: E55 CLOSED (lead's request, 11:35 PM).** Step 1 is the whole of what ran: the people model it trained (70.1% / CE 0.835 vs 67.1% / 0.909 on 668 holdout games) is the recipe E56's refresh adopted, and it replaced models/human/net.npz on Oct 19, 12:50 PM; it is the model bot8 plays (E57) and the opponent in E68/E71. It fails as a hoarder (0.40 cards held at turn 60+ vs people's 1.37, 48 games), so the self-play seat kept E27's patch. Step 2 (50% of training games against it with E54's machinery) never started: E54 ran as a pool of past nets, and the lead closed E54 as a production candidate (Oct 20, 8:45 PM); the "learn against an opponent" line continues as E60 phase 1, waiting on E70. Nothing to revert. The ledger said "running" since Oct 19 only because the closing line wasn't written.

**Which first: E54**, with the human-style seat as one pool member. It tests the fixed-point reading directly, it is cheap per game, and B by self-play has already run for 40+ rounds (E13, E27) without effect; B's new part (a stronger way to learn from a people model) needs E54's machinery anyway. E55 follows only if its people model beats today's on the holdout.

**Literature (from memory, primary sources, not re-checked today):**
- Tesauro 1995, "Temporal Difference Learning and TD-Gammon", Communications of the ACM 38(3): self-play learning without search reached top human level in backgammon, a dice game.
- Li et al. 2020, "Suphx: Mastering Mahjong with Deep Reinforcement Learning", arXiv:2003.13590: cloning people first, then policy-gradient self-play; a predicted final reward to tame luck in the reward. The closest template for E54's dice noise and for E55.
- Vinyals et al. 2019, "Grandmaster level in StarCraft II using multi-agent reinforcement learning", Nature 575: cloning from human games, then RL against a league of past agents with a KL to the human policy (E54's pool, E55's model).
- Berner et al. 2019, "Dota 2 with Large Scale Deep Reinforcement Learning" (OpenAI Five), arXiv:1912.06680: PPO self-play, 80% against the latest net and 20% against past ones.
- Perolat et al. 2022, "Mastering the game of Stratego with model-free multiagent reinforcement learning", Science 378: expert level with no search at play.
- Jacob et al. 2022, "Modeling Strong and Human-Like Gameplay with KL-Regularized Search", ICML; Bakhtin et al. 2023, "Mastering the Game of No-Press Diplomacy via Human-Regularized Reinforcement Learning and Planning", ICLR: a KL toward a human-cloned policy made agents better against people.
- Catan: Szita, Chaslot and Spronck 2010, "Monte-Carlo Tree Search in Settlers of Catan", ACG 2009 (LNCS 6048); Dobre and Lascarides 2015, "Online learning and mining human play in complex games", IEEE CIG (people's moves to guide search in Catan); Gendre and Kaneko 2020, "Playing Catan with Cross-dimensional Neural Network", arXiv:2008.07079 (deep RL without search, reported against JSettlers' agents).
- Schulman et al. 2017, "Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms", arXiv:1707.06347.

## E56 — training: people's moves with a known development-card hand go into training (owner's decision Oct 19, 9:45 AM; lead's brief 9:50 AM; PREPARED Oct 19, 10:05 AM Toronto)
- **Why:** gap 1 (late live error 0.41-0.44 against 0.34 in self-play). Until now human_dataset.py kept only the person's turns with no unplayed development card, so the people data skipped every turn where a person sat on a card, which is most of the late game. live-loss's export (human_data.DevHands) backfills the hand from later plays and the end-of-game reveal and keeps a turn when only one hand fits.
- **Same bet as E14, with the missing half of the data.** E14 (people's moves from games they won, HUMAN_POLICY_REPEAT=3) raised move prediction 55% to 63% and cut late bias 0.02, but showed no live effect: round 73 went 235-165 (58.8%) against round 66's 216-149 (59.2%) on the same accounts. E56 repeats E14 at the same weight with the card-holding turns added; a null here closes the "people's moves as targets" line.
- **Data (counted Oct 19, 9:58 AM):** train/policy_won 49,344 moves, 5,787 at turn 60+ (11.7%); train/policy_won_exact 84,400 moves, 22,252 at turn 60+ (26.4%), so 1.7x the moves and 3.8x the late ones. Holdout opponent 19,730 rows (9.4% late) → opponent_exact 31,746 (23.0% late), same 665 games. At HUMAN_POLICY_REPEAT=3 the people's share of move targets rises about 1.7x.
- **Change:** (1) train.sh reads HUMAN_POLICY_DIR (default data/human/train/policy_won); workers.env sets HUMAN_POLICY_DIR=data/human/train/policy_won_exact, HUMAN_POLICY_REPEAT stays 3. Needs a safe restart. (2) human_dataset.sh's background refresh of the people model trains and scores on opponent_exact (OPPONENT_SHARDS, default opponent_exact). It runs before every round, so it is live as soon as it lands; it replaces models/human/net.npz only if the new model predicts the new holdout at least as well. The self-play seat uses the holddev file (E27), not models/human/net.npz, so (2) changes no self-play until the holddev check below says so.
- **Caveat:** until live-loss lands its human_dataset.py, the round's data step adds new games only to the old folders; the _exact folders stay a snapshot of 9:51 AM (about 1,900 won games). Fine for 6 rounds; live-loss to land within the day.
- **Runs next to E51** (data, not search). Attribution: E51's first 800-simulation round is 125; its verdict pools rounds 133-138. E56's rounds are the ones trained after its restart (written below once applied); E51 rounds before that have the old data.
- **Verdict rule (written before the restart):**
  1. Gate and guardrails as usual (a promotion is the signal).
  2. Live value error by phase on the holdout (the loop's "live value MSE" line) must not rise: the mean of E56's first 3 rounds against rounds 120-122 (late 0.408 / 0.411 / 0.411, middle 0.509-0.511, overall 0.576-0.578). A rise of 0.010+ in late or overall reverts.
  3. People model: the refreshed model's holdout move accuracy and cross-entropy on holdout/opponent_exact against today's model (measured below). The holddev patch (E27's -3 bias) stays until research's E55 check (cards held at turn 60+, bias 0, must fall near people's 1.37) says it can go.
  4. Over E56's first 6 rounds, the mean gate against round 73 compared with E51's rounds 125 up to the restart (without the data) and with the 49.9% of rounds 111-121. Below 49.5% with no promotion reverts.
- **Revert:** remove HUMAN_POLICY_DIR from workers.env and safe_restart_training.sh; set OPPONENT_SHARDS=opponent in human_dataset.sh's call or revert its line, and if the model was replaced, copy back data/orchestrator/training/human_net_pre_e56.npz (today's model, saved 10:05 AM) to models/human/net.npz.
- **Before (Oct 19, 10:10 AM), on holdout/opponent_exact (31,746 moves, 665 games):** uniform 26.6% / 2.100; round 73 (main) 61.6% / 1.112; today's people model (models/human/net.npz) 67.1% / 0.909; the holddev seat (E27) 67.3% / 0.911. So the -3 bias costs no prediction here; dropping it is decided by E55's cards-held check, not by this score.
- **Applied Oct 19, 10:18 AM:** landed 9b9b20b (797 tests); HUMAN_POLICY_DIR set in workers.env; safe restart waiting for round 124's match. The people-model refresh change is live from the next round's data step (no restart needed). Expected first E56-trained round: 125, which is also E51's first 800-simulation round. So no E51 round runs without E56; E51's 800-sim share of the 18-round window starts at 1/18 and grows, while E56's data is in full from round 125. Read rounds 125-127 mostly as E56, rounds 133-138 as both. Baseline for both: rounds 111-122 (49.9%).
- **Restarted Oct 19, 10:53 AM (RUNNING):** the loop resumed at round 125's training with HUMAN_POLICY_DIR=policy_won_exact and --simulations 800. Round 125 is confirmed as the first E56-trained round (and E51's first 800-sim data). Last rounds before it: r123 50.88%/8000, r124 46.40%/1000 (early fail); live MSE r123 0.578 (0.892/0.511/0.410), r124 0.576 (0.890/0.508/0.409). Live MSE 0.578 (0.890 / 0.510 / 0.412) against rounds 120-122's 0.576-0.578 and late 0.408-0.411: flat, within noise (1 of 3 rounds). Round 126's self-play (800 sims) was done before round 125's match ended, so E51 cost no time this round. People-model refresh on opponent_exact still training (CPU, slow while the match ran); research's E55 copy of the same recipe scored 70.1% / 0.835 against 67.1% / 0.909, so it should replace models/human/net.npz.

## E57 — live-play: a new account (bot8) plays the people model with our normal search (owner's request Oct 19, 12:30 PM; lead's brief 12:40-12:55 PM; PREPARED Oct 19, 1:25 PM Toronto)
- **Hypothesis:** a net that imitates people (models/human/net.npz, the opponent model, about 70% top-1 on people's held-out moves) with our 2400-simulation search is a yardstick: it reaches some rating X on colonist, and its record by opponent band against round 73's accounts says how much of our play is "people's moves plus search" and how much is self-play. No expectation of beating round 73.
- **Setup:** bot8 on bot VM 2 (ubuntu@3.18.220.56, next to bot3 and bot4), PORT 8768, SIMULATIONS 2400, RANKED_AFTER 5, no OPPONENT_MODEL; NET_PATH=/home/ubuntu/colonist-ai/models/human/net.npz, NET_NAME=people model (ranked_runner --net-path, landed 31c4c74). The runner reinstalls the net between games when sync_bot.sh brings a retrained one; each game's label is "people model, <file date>" and its games.jsonl row has fixed_net: true.
- **Metric:** rating after 100 ranked games, and win rate by opponent band (under 2100, 2100-2300, 2300+) against bots 3, 4, 5, 7 over the same days. 100 games give ±10%.
- **Cost:** a third account on bot VM 2 takes some simulations per move from bot3 and bot4 (live-loss: a small effect); watched in their records.
- **Revert:** stop the service (bot_ctl.sh stop-account bot8, then systemctl disable colonist-casual@bot8 on the VM).
- **Offline check (Oct 19, 1:00-1:25 PM, coordinator, 1 core each, snapshot of the people model from 12:50 PM):** people@200 vs round73@200: 7-13 (35% ± 21, 20 games). people@200 vs people@0 (raw policy): 6-14 then 18-22, pooled 24-36 (40% ± 13, 60 games). Control, round73@200 vs round73@0: 8-12 (40% ± 20, 20 games). So at 200 simulations search loses to the raw policy for both nets alike; the people model's value head (val value 0.657) is not clearly worse to search with than round 73's. Not broken; fine to deploy as a yardstick. (Side finding for research: 200-sim search below the raw policy for round 73 too, 20 games only.)
- **Signed in** Oct 19, 1:25-1:42 PM (owner over VNC, lead's script): username Vasos5306. Landed c4bd87d (2:00 PM): bot8=Vasos5306 in status.py, rank_watch.py and human_dataset.sh's own accounts (its games stay out of the people dataset).
- **Waiting on:** bot8.env on bot VM 2 (live-play has no SSH; draft at data/orchestrator/scratch/live-play/bot8.env), enabling colonist-casual@bot8, and Vasos5306 in own_accounts.env on both bot VMs. The NET_PATH option is landed, so bot8 plays the people model from its first game.

## E58 — live-play: fix, the first seat's first settlement gets its full thinking time (found Oct 19, 1:30 PM; LIVE Oct 19, 1:42 PM Toronto, landed 7efcc77, each account from the end of its current game; all four accounts, a bug fix)
- **Finding (clock table, last 200 ranked games per account, 800 games):** the bridge read colonist's timer only from game updates, never from the game's first snapshot. When the bot moves first, its first settlement comes before any update, so it ran a plain 2400-simulation search: 501 of 501 first-seat games, median 5.3 s and 2400 simulations, against 20.4 s and 11,588 simulations for the second seat's first settlement (499 games). BUDGETS meant 20 s and up to 20,000 for both. The snapshot carries startTime and allocatedTime 60 (checked in 6 captures).
- **Fix:** bridge.py notes the timer from the new game's snapshot and drops the previous game's (a stale, expired deadline could otherwise give 0.05 s). Test: test_game_start_snapshot_sets_the_first_settlements_timer.
- **Hypothesis:** first-seat games gain 1-3 points (E18: about 1-2 points per doubling of simulations, more for the first seat; this is about 2 doublings on the most important single decision).
- **Metric:** first-seat win rate after the fix against before: 63.1% (316-185, last 250 ranked games per account, ±4.4%). Second seat as the control: 53.7% (268-231). At least 300 first-seat games after the fix (about 600 games, 2 days on four accounts); the first-seat first settlement must show about 20 s and 10,000+ simulations in bridge.log from the first game.
- **Revert:** git revert of the fix, sync_bot.sh, safe restarts. Nothing to revert in settings.
- **Note:** a bug fix to the intended budget, not a new setting, so it goes to all four accounts at once and does not block the one-experiment rule; task 2's clock experiment on bot4 starts after it.
- **VERDICT, Oct 21, 12:05 AM Toronto (live-play): KEPT, CLOSED. Fix works; no win-rate gain that 317 games can see.** Ranked games from scratch/live-play/open_rows.jsonl (all accounts, through Oct 20, 10:39 PM), cut at the fix (Oct 19, 1:42 PM): first seat 202-115 (63.7% ± 5.3) after against 1,345-884 (60.3% ± 2.0) before, +3.4 points; second seat (control) 179-136 (56.8% ± 5.5) after against 53.8% before, +3.0. The first-seat edge over the second seat is 6.9 points after against 6.5 before, so the gain matches the control (the hypothesis' 1-3 points is below what 317 games resolve). Against the ledger's own baseline (63.1%) +0.6. The fix stays: the first settlement now gets its intended clock (11 of 12 checked at once, and E72's openings show 8,000-20,000 simulations). Confound: nets and accounts changed over the window (rounds 152-168, round 73 pins, bot8-bot12). Script: scratch/live-play/e58_verdict.py.
- **Cross-check, Oct 21, 9:25 AM Toronto (live-loss): like with like, same verdict.** Only the four round 73 accounts (bot3/4/5/7, label round 73) after the fix, against the last 2,000 ranked games before it (Oct 16-19, almost all round 73), through Oct 21, 8:59 AM: first seat 140-77 (64.5%, n 217) against 63.4% (400-231) before, +1.1 points [-6.3, +8.5]; second seat 110-95 (53.7%, n 205) against 54.4% (344-288), -0.7 [-8.6, +7.2]. Seat gap change +1.8 [-9.0, +12.6]. No gain shown, as live-play found; 217 games resolve about ±7 points, so a 1-3 point gain stays invisible. All accounts after the fix (367 first-seat games): 66.8% vs 58.9% second. Script: scratch/live-loss/seat_split.py 2000 (now lists bot9-bot12 and prints each account and net after the fix).

When it calls itself 20-60% from turn 20 against 2100+ players it wins 22.1% (round 152 26.5%, round 73 30.6%): it is too hopeful when behind against strong people. kl 0.1042, val value 0.090 (the iter-29 uptick did not continue). **By the lead's rule** (52%+ AND holdout no worse) it is not a candidate seed, so E54 would close. **Research's view:** the gate gain is the biggest since round 73 and real (lower bound 54%), while the holdout measures value error on people's positions, not play; the live gap says they can disagree. So on people's games the wide net is level to slightly worse, not better. Equal think time (wide 140 vs round 73 200): wide **45.4% ± 3.0** over 1000 games (454-546), 340 vs 358 ms a move, so the time match was fair and the wide net is clearly worse per second. ## E59 — research: a softer self-play move target (lead, Oct 19, 2:50 PM; PROPOSED, offline first)
- **Why:** mcts.py's root target is improved_policy = softmax(logits + sigma(completed Q)), sigma = (c_visit 50 + max N) * c_scale 1.0 / 2 * (q + 1). At a 400-sim root with max N about 200, a Q gap of 0.1 is 12.5 nats: the target is near one-hot on the move with the higher search Q, while two 400-sim searches of the same position pick a different top move 10.5% of the time with near one-hot flips (E51's measurement). The net can only fit contradictory one-hot targets as a blur, which matches round 73 sitting KL 0.41 from its own targets after training on them.
- **Source:** Danihelka, Guez, Schrittwieser, Silver 2022, Policy improvement by planning with Gumbel (ICLR): the sigma scale is a free parameter; KataGo's policy target at a few hundred playouts is the visit distribution, softer than this.
- **Change (offline):** a target_scale option in mcts.py for root_targets only (default = c_scale, so nothing changes); pick() keeps c_scale.
- **Metric:** on the 314-position paired set (e51_kl.py play): top-1 disagreement and KL between two independent 400-sim searches' targets at scales 1, 0.5, 0.25, 0.1, and each target's KL to the prior (too close means nothing to learn). A scale that halves the disagreement while keeping most of the distance from the prior goes to a production trial (training) after E51's verdict: the pooled gate over 6 rounds against 49.9%.
- **Cost:** fleet match windows, under an hour. **Revert:** nothing live.
- **Oct 19, 3:00 PM (research): option landed, measurement running.** mcts.py Search(target_scale=None) (None = c_scale, so production is unchanged) and root_targets(root, scale=); test in tests/colonist/test_search_chance.py; landed 0d67641. Measurement: scratch/research/e59_scale.py, e51_kl.py play's procedure without the 800 search (so the positions are a new draw, not the same 314), each sampled position searched twice at 400, and both roots' targets taken at scales 1, 0.5, 0.25, 0.1 from the same searches. 3 x 8 games (seeds 7510000000, ...08, ...16), about 900 positions, niced on the coordinator rather than the fleet (about 15 min of 3 cores). Job jobs/e59.log; rows in data/orchestrator/e59/.
- **Oct 19, 3:45 PM (research): measured.** 24 games, 1,101 positions, each searched twice at 400 (actual run about 45 min, not 15). Means with 95% intervals (pooled by scratch/research/e59_pool.py):

  | scale | KL between the two searches' targets | top-1 agree | KL target to prior (reach) | target entropy | noise / reach |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 1.0 (production) | 0.303 ±0.093 | 91.4% ±1.7 | 0.257 ±0.030 | 0.50 | 1.18 |
  | 0.5 | 0.125 ±0.045 | 94.1% ±1.4 | 0.148 ±0.018 | 0.58 | 0.84 |
  | 0.25 | 0.048 ±0.020 | 94.9% ±1.3 | 0.075 ±0.010 | 0.66 | 0.64 |
  | 0.1 | 0.012 ±0.006 | 96.9% ±1.0 | 0.024 ±0.004 | 0.74 | 0.51 |

  Read: at production's scale the noise between two searches (0.30) is larger than what the target says beyond the prior (0.26). Halving the scale cuts the noise 59% and keeps 58% of the reach; top-1 disagreement 8.6% -> 5.9% (-31%; not halved: part of it is genuine near-ties). Lower scales cut noise faster than reach, but at 0.25 the target keeps only 29% of the reach and at 0.1 9% (almost nothing to learn). The noise is concentrated: median KL between searches is 0.0004 at every scale, so a few positions (near-ties with large flips) carry the mean. Caveat: agreement at low scales rises partly because both targets lean on the same prior. **Recommendation: scale 0.5 for the production trial** after E51's verdict (Search(target_scale=0.5) in self-play only; pick() unchanged), judged by the pooled gate over 6 rounds against 49.9%. 0.25 is the fallback if 0.5 is flat. Baseline check: E51's earlier draw gave 89.5% agree and KL 0.379 at scale 1, inside these intervals.

## E60 — research: a league for self-play, with an exploiter of round 73 (PLAN ONLY, Oct 19, 3:30 PM; lead's request 3:15 PM; not built, the lead decides)
- **What production already has (train.sh, loop.py defaults):** a small league. The human seat is drawn first at 15% (E13, now the hold-dev model); the rest go to an older opponent at --opponent-rate 0.2 (loop.py's default; train.sh doesn't set it), picked uniformly from the 3 rounds before the best (r70, r71, r72) plus AB:2 and F. So about 68% pure self-play, 15% human seat, 3.4% each for r70, r71, r72, AB:2, F. Because round 73 has been best for 55 rounds, the net members have been frozen at r70-72, nets within a few points of r73 (r73 beat r66 52.8%): the pool barely differs from self-play. Nothing in it is trained to find r73's holes.
- **Hypothesis:** the plateau is partly a blind spot of self-play: the net only meets its own style, so weaknesses its own search doesn't exploit never get targets. An exploiter trained only to beat frozen r73 finds those holes (if any); games against it, and against a wider spread of past nets, give the main net targets self-play never produces.
- **Sources:** league: Vinyals et al. 2019, Grandmaster level in StarCraft II using multi-agent reinforcement learning, Nature 575 (main agents, main exploiters trained only against the current main agent, league exploiters, prioritized fictitious self-play over a pool of frozen past agents). Exploiter as a best response, pool as its average: Heinrich, Lanctot, Silver 2015, Fictitious Self-Play in Extensive-Form Games (ICML); generalised as PSRO in Lanctot et al. 2017, A Unified Game-Theoretic Approach to Multiagent Reinforcement Learning (NeurIPS).
- **Design, in 2 phases (phase 1 only if phase 0 passes):**
  - **Phase 0, the exploiter as a diagnostic (offline, no loop.py change).** A scratch driver like e54_dist.py: self-play with net = exploiter (starts as r73), --opponents round_073.npz --opponent-rate 1 (selfplay.py already records only the main net's moves in such games), 3,500 games at 400 sims per iteration; train.py from the exploiter on its last 2 iterations, 2 epochs, lr 3e-4, EMA as production; check vs r73 at 200 sims, 1,000 games. Up to 5 iterations. **Verdict:** an exploiter at 55%+ over 2,000 games (±2.2%) means r73 has a hole that self-play doesn't see; under 52% after 5 iterations means there's no cheap hole and phase 1's exploiter slot is dropped (the past-net pool alone is then close to what E54 tests). Either answer is worth having: it says whether more diverse opponents can help at all.
  - **Phase 1, the league in production's loop.** Shares (fixed, by seed): self-play 55%, human seat 15% (unchanged), exploiter 10%, r73 8%, r66 5%, r56 3%, r40 2%, r13 2%. Code: loop.py --league FILE (JSON list of net and share, read every round, so the exploiter can be swapped in without a restart; replaces the "3 rounds before best" rule when given); selfplay.seat_plan picks the opponent by weight instead of uniformly; distributed.py already copies .npz opponents to each host. The exploiter keeps training on the side (phase 0's driver, against the current best) and replaces its pool slot whenever its new version beats the best at 55%+. About half a day of code plus tests; off by default.
- **Metric:** (1) the E38 gate vs r73, pooled over 6 rounds against the 49.9% baseline (as E51); (2) a pool match: the candidate vs every pool member, 500 games each at 200 sims, its worst result must not fall below r73's against the same member (league play should remove holes, not just lift the average); (3) late live error (loop live MSE late phase) not worse than 0.412; (4) self-play minutes per round.
- **Fleet cost per round:** a game against a searched net costs the same as a self-play game but records half the positions. Searched-net opponent share goes from about 10% (r70-72) to 30% (exploiter plus 5 past nets), so a round needs about 12% more games for the same positions: about +1.5 min at 400 sims, +3-4 min at E51's 800. The exploiter's own iterations cost a round's self-play each (12-30 min of fleet, depending on sims) plus 1,000 check games; run every 3rd round, about +30% fleet on average, or on the PC plus 2 spot hosts as E54 does. Phase 0 alone: 5 iterations x (about 25 min self-play on 2 hosts + 10 min GPU + 1,000 games) = about 5 h on E54's setup, no money.
- **Biggest risks:** (a) the weak pool members (r13, r40) give easy wins, and their games' value targets teach "this position is winning" more often than it is against a strong player: that is exactly the late overconfidence we have against people. Mitigation: keep their share at 2-3%, or give those games policy targets only (a train.py weight; needs an opponent field in the shards, today only human_opponent is recorded). (b) Slower rounds (above). (c) The exploiter may find a narrow trick (a robber or trade-free tempo pattern) that the main net learns to stop, without any gain against people; metric (2) and live play are the check. (d) E13 was null at 15%: a different opponent alone did not move the late error; this plan bets on the exploiter's targeted pressure, which E13 lacked.
- **Relation to E54/E55:** E54 tests a pool of past nets with policy-gradient learning and no search; E60 is the same idea inside the existing search loop. If E54 shows a gain against r73, E60 phase 1 is its cheaper production path; phase 0 is independent and can run after E54 frees the 2 hosts.
- **Cost:** no money; phase 0 is about 5 h of PC GPU and 2 fleet hosts. **Revert:** nothing live until phase 1; phase 1 reverts by dropping --league.
- **Phase 0 approved (lead, Oct 19, 9 PM), after E54 ends or stops; driver ready Oct 19, 9:10 PM.** The approved home is the GPU VM's between-round slot (one job; E54 holds it now). The 400-sim design doesn't fit there: a production self-play game costs about 11 s of a core, so 3,500 games are about 80 min on the VM's 8 cores, and its 1,000-game 200-sim check about 1.5 h, against a slot of about 15 min. So phase 0 uses E54's machinery instead: search-free games (0.2-0.28 s a game), the learner against round 73 in every game (pool round 73 only, no self-play, no people seat), policy-gradient update from round 73 with KL to round 73 (lr 3e-5, kl 0.02, as E54). 20,000 games an iteration (about 1.8M rows; smoke: 40 games, 90 rows a game, only the learner's moves recorded), one iteration per promotion match (about 11 min of the slot, as E54), 10 iterations, raw vs raw against round 73 every 2 iterations (1,000 games). **Verdict:** 55%+ raw vs raw means round 73's prior has an exploitable hole; then the 200-sim check on the fleet (gate.py, 2,000 games, about 25 min of fleet, the lead's time) says whether the search covers it. Under 52% after 10 iterations: no cheap hole, the exploiter slot is dropped. Caveat: a raw-net hole may be one the search covers; only the searched check counts for phase 1. Driver: scratch/research/e60_dist.py (e54_dist.py with name e60, VM folder /home/owen/e60, own gpu.lock tag); start: jobs/e60-run.sh, log jobs/e60.log, readings e60/run1/eval.txt. **Revert:** kill_job.sh e60 --yes, then remove the VM's gpu.lock only if it holds e60-iter-N.
- **Oct 20, 8:50 PM Toronto (training): phase 1 mechanics with E54's net as the exploiter (lead's question 8:43 PM; nothing built, nothing live).**
  - **(a) --reference takes an .npz today.** loop.py adds the --reference entries to the opponent list as they are; selfplay.load_opponent loads any entry ending in .npz as a searched net, and distributed.py copies each .npz opponent to every host (opponent_N.npz). So `--reference AB:2,F,<path>/net.npz` in train.sh works with no code change. The file must sit on the coordinator (E54's iteration 30 is not under data/orchestrator/e54/run1 here; copy it to models/ first).
  - **(b) How it would play.** An opponent net plays with exactly the main net's search: the 400/24 mix (25% of moves at 400 sims, the rest at 24, the opening at full search), Gumbel noise and all. There is no per-opponent setting. Only the main net's moves are recorded, with the usual targets (search policy, search value, game result z, aux). The exploiter's own moves give no rows.
  - **(c) Cost.** The opponent pick is uniform: today 17% of games (20% of the 85% left after the 15% human seat) are split 5 ways (rounds 149-151, AB:2, F), 3.4% each. Adding the exploiter as a 6th entry gives it only 2.8%. At 10% of games: a game with one opponent seat records half the rows, so the round has about 6% fewer rows (84% to 79% of the most possible); keeping rows level needs about 300 more games, about +2 min of self-play. At the production mix the exploiter costs no extra search. At a flat 200 sims a move it costs more than the main net's average of about 118 sims a move: about 1.35 times a self-play game, so +3.5% fleet, about +1 min. Total about +3 min of self-play a round. Self-play now finishes early, alongside the previous round's training and gate (33-65 min), so the round itself should not get longer.
  - **(d) Smallest change for 10% at 200 sims.** Without code: list the exploiter 7 times in --reference (7 of 12 entries times 17% is about 10%), at the production mix. That works but is clumsy (7 copies to every host). Cleaner, about 30 lines plus tests, off by default: `--exploiter FILE --exploiter-rate 0.10 --exploiter-simulations 200` in loop.py and selfplay.py, drawn first like the human seat (seat_plan), with _Game.next_search giving that seat a flat search (no noise, no fast moves) when its agent is the exploiter; distributed.py copies the file like human_opponent. The loop reads it every round, so a newer exploiter can replace it without a restart if loop.py reads the path from a file. E60's full --league design (weights for every past net) is the larger version (half a day).

## E61 — live-play: more of the clock for the decisions that matter, on Sherie3424 (bot4) only (lead's brief Oct 19, 2:50 PM; LIVE Oct 19, 3:25 PM Toronto, each account between games)
- **What:** bot4's env file sets THINK_BUDGETS=PLAY_TURN=0.3:10:5,MOVE_ROBBER=0.5:10:5,ROLL=0.33:2:0.6667 (landed bfab8a9; bridge.budgets_from_env). A turn's actions: cap 5 s -> 10 s and 4,800 -> 12,000 sims; the 30% share of the time left stays, so the first action of a turn gets about 8.3 s (30 s clock), later ones less. Robber: cap 5 -> 10 s, share 0.4 -> 0.5 (a 15 s robber timer gives 6.25 s instead of 5.0; 0.4 alone would never pass 5 s). Roll with a choice: 1 s / 800 -> 2 s / 1,600 (in practice about 1.8 s: 33% of an 8 s timer). Opening and discards unchanged. Numbered E61 because research's league plan took E60.
- **Why:** the clock table (task 1): a turn's actions use 5.1 s of a 30 s+ allowance, 83% stopped by the 5 s cap; the remaining-time limits bind under 1%. The time is there and unused. E18: a doubling of sims is worth 1-2 points in self-play, more for decisions that matter.
- **Baseline:** E58 counts as part of the baseline on all four accounts. bot4 was the weakest account over the last 24 h (53-51, 2181). Controls: bot3, bot5, bot7 over the same days. Confound: bot4 shares bot VM 2 with bot3 (and bot8 until it moves), and both lost about 20% of search speed since 2:35 PM; longer thinking on bot4 also overlaps more with bot3's searches.
- **Metric and verdict:** bot4's ranked win rate from 3:25 PM against the three controls' pooled win rate over the same window, at least 300 bot4 games (about 3 days at 100/day), split by opponent band (under 2100, 2100-2300, 2300+), plus bot4's rating move. Keep and extend to all accounts if bot4 beats the controls by 3 points or more; revert if it trails them by 3 or more; in between, extend to 600 games or call it no effect. Also check: no lost-on-time games (turn thinking p90 stays under 25 s), per-turn thinking from clock_table.py.
- **Revert:** delete the THINK_BUDGETS line from scripts/vm/bot/accounts/bot4.env on bot VM 2, then safe_restart_account.sh bot4.
- **Next:** the opponent-model test on Dale7112 waits until E61 has its games (one live experiment at a time).
- **Start time correction (Oct 19, 3:30 PM):** at 3:26 PM bot4 (and bot3) had not restarted yet: their game ended 3:17 PM, before the 3:18 PM restart request, so the restart waits for the end of the game that began about 3:18 PM. The real start is the restart time in data/bot4/restart-orchestrator.log on bot VM 2; count games from there. **Real start: Oct 19, 3:29 PM** (19:29:27 UTC; bridge.log shows "think budgets from THINK_BUDGETS").
- **First look (Oct 19, 7:50 PM Toronto, 14 bot4 ranked games, noise):** bot4 11-3; controls 43-24 (64.2%; bot3 12-9, bot5 20-4, bot7 11-11). clock_table.py bot4 14: PLAY_TURN median 8.4 s, 4,323 sims (bot3 on the same VM: 5.1 s, 2,987), turn thinking p90 23.7 s, max 40.8 s (bot3 max 41.4 s); robber median 6.6 s, roll 1.8 s. **Problem:** two robber-after-a-7 searches stalled on bot VM 2 (16.1 s with 1,808 sims at 3:54 PM; 12.5 s with 1,280 sims later, normal is about 650 sims/s): the budget was about 7.5 s (0.5 of the 15 s robber timer) and the overshoot 5-9 s. The 16 s one passed colonist's timer, colonist placed the robber, and the bridge stopped (game led at 78%). The baseline's 5 s would have survived both stalls (13.6 s, 10 s). Knight robbers (10.2-10.6 s, inside the turn timer) are fine. A second bot4 stop (5:19 PM, monopoly not shown within 15 s, the bot was at 2-9%) is not clock-related. **Count stops as losses:** stopped games are missing from games.jsonl (neither capture is there); stops.py since the start: bot4 2, controls 0 (last 24 h: bot4 3, bot3 2, bot5 0, bot7 0). Adjusted bot4: 11-5. **Proposed change (needs someone with VM 2 access):** bot4 MOVE_ROBBER=0.4:10:5 (a 7's robber back to 5-6 s, knight robbers keep up to 10 s); restart between games. Step 2 after a day with no stuck queue: all accounts, plus a restart of colonist-spectator on both VMs (for queue null while waiting). bot4's E61 sees a small extra wait only.

## Research note — the net's hopefulness near even against people (Oct 19, 5:15 PM Toronto; analysis only, no E-number)
- **Question (training's 4:44 PM finding):** search values are too hopeful when the game is even or behind. Is it the raw net, and is it tied to some kind of position a value fix could target?
- **Raw net, round 73, 832 ranked games (32,482 positions on the bot's turn):** yes, the raw net does it. Turn 20-60, value near 0: stated 49.9%, won 41.3%; late (60+): stated 50.0%, won 34.0%. Clear leads are calibrated (value above 0.6: stated 94-95%, won 92-95%).
- **Split by feature (turn 20+, value -0.3 to 0.3, 4,020 positions, 534 games; game-clustered 95% intervals):** gap -10.6% ± 5.0 overall, and about the same in every slice: public VP difference -2 to +3 (-8 to -14%), our hidden VP cards 0/1/2, their dev cards 0-3, either hand size 2-10, largest army or longest road with us/them/nobody, their VP 3-9. No slice is near zero; none stands out beyond its interval (their 3+ dev cards -16% ± 10 is the largest).
- **People against people (holdout, 686 games, 3,326 such positions):** the same kind of offset, -6.6% ± 5.4. The bot's -10.6% is not reliably worse (difference about 4 ± 7 points).
- **Reading:** a flat offset in the middle of the value range, not a blind spot for one kind of position. Between moves of the same position a flat offset cancels, so it barely changes which move the search picks. A value-only fix (train the value on people's even positions) would shift the curve and most likely not win games; E16 and E23 already showed value changes don't move live results. What matters is what the offset stands for: people play even positions better than the net's picture of them (it models the opponent as itself). That is E55/E57's ground (a people model as the opponent in search), not a value target.
- **Decision:** no value experiment proposed. Tools: scratch/research/raw_cal.py NET, scratch/research/gap_split.py NET [FOLDER] (about 1 min on CPU each).
