## E64 — research: does round 73 aim the robber well? rehex's rule as a yardstick (Oct 19, 10:10 PM Toronto; offline, main VM CPU)
- **Origin:** openhex's rehex report (analysis/2026-10-20-rehex-rules.md): rehex's robber goes to the hex with max (opponent pips - own pips, cities x2), never its own, 509/513. Suggested as an input feature ("opponent's next robber target") and as a fixed-rule opponent.
- **Hypothesis:** if raw round 73 already picks rule-best hexes, the feature is redundant; if it doesn't and the rule wins raw games, the net misses an easy quantity.
- **Step 1, agreement (done):** scratch/research/robber_rule.py, 300 self-play games of raw r73 (moves sampled, seeds 7,000,000+). Net's top robber tile (probability summed over victims) is rule-best in 4156/7125 = 58.3% ± 1.1; mean policy mass on rule-best tiles 45.8%; shortfall 4+ pips in 23.4%; own hex while avoidable 727/7125 (10%). Caveat: the rule ignores what can be stolen and which resource the opponent needs, so disagreement is not automatically a mistake.
- **Step 2, match (done Oct 19, 10:17 PM):** scratch/research/robber_match.py 2000: raw r73 with robber tile restricted to rule-best tiles (victim from the net) vs raw r73, seats alternate, seeds 8,000,000+. Verdict rule: above 53% the net's robber is weak; 47-53% no gap. **Result: 1000-1000 = 50.0% ± 2.2.**
- **Verdict: CLOSED, no gap.** The 42% disagreement costs nothing in raw play; the robber-target input feature is dropped. (Search at play time only narrows the gap further.)
- **Cost:** CPU only, no production change.

## E65 — research: teaching road plans that take several turns (PLAN, Oct 19, 10:55 PM Toronto; owner's question 10:32 PM via the lead, who called it E63, a number live-play's Dale7112 trial already holds)
- **Question:** how does the net learn plans like "spend three roads to join two networks for Longest Road"? A 3-road plan spans 2-4 turns with dice between them; at 2,400 simulations through chance nodes the search does not see its payoff, so only the value head can, and it must recognise the situation from the board.
- **What we have today (checked in code):** 4 auxiliary targets, all at the game's end, for the player to move: Longest Road holder (+1 me, -1 them, 0 nobody), Largest Army holder (same), my final points / 15, their final points / 15. MSE, weight 0.1 (train.py --aux-weight default, train.sh relies on it), a two-layer head off the global vector (aux1, aux2), graph nets only. Nothing in the inputs summarises reachable road length; the net sees roads per edge and must work it out through 5 rounds of message passing (a 3-road join can be 6+ edges long).
- **Prototype (done, scratch/research/e63_plan.py, 30 random-player games, 1,190 calls):** per player, longest road now and with 1/2/3 more roads (trail through own roads plus up to k empty land edges, stopped by enemy buildings), empty edges needed to join two own networks (cap 4), empty edges to the nearest free legal settlement spot (cap 4). Longest-road-now matches the engine's in 1,190/1,190. Pure Python cost per player: mean 1.39 ms, median 0.36, p99 10.5, max 49.5 (random games have more roads than real ones). Search runs about 1.3 ms per simulation live (700-800 sims/s), so uncached features for both players at every leaf would cut simulations by about two thirds. Needs a cache keyed on roads + buildings (most leaves in one search share them) and/or a bitmask rewrite; target under 10% speed loss, measured before any match.
- **Plan, in order (each step stops the line if it fails):**
  1. **Step 0, probe (CPU only, about 2 h, no code landed):** on the human holdout (636 games, about 25k positions) and one self-play round, (a) does round 73's global vector already encode the plan numbers (linear probe, R² by feature; E64 showed the net can already know what a rule computes), and (b) do the plan numbers explain the game result beyond round 73's value (regress z on v plus plan features, game-clustered cross-validation, turn 30+ and the even band). Go on only if (b) lowers the error by 0.003+ on people's positions or the probe R² for the 3-road gain and join distance is under 0.5. Data: shards keep the board per row (static map + dynamic roads/buildings), the game index and the turn, so this needs no new self-play.
  2. **Step A, plan features as inputs (offline, the E40 design):** a new optional weight plan_in (12 numbers → global vector) added to the first global layer, zero at start, so a net made from round 73 computes exactly what round 73 does and old nets load unchanged (the same trick as the optional aux weights). Features computed in the encoder at play time (with the cache) and, for training, precomputed once from the shards' boards (about 3 h single-core per 4M rows, about 25 min on 8 cores; cached next to the data). Arms from round 73 on rounds' data, 3 legs of 4M, 2 epochs, shared seeds: control vs features. Training about 7 min an epoch on the PC GPU, so about 1 h an arm. Code: encode/graph/train changes plus tests, about one task-day.
  3. **Step B, 10-turns-ahead targets (same run, extra arms):** my and their longest-road length 10 turns later (/15) and who holds Longest Road 10 turns later, as a separate optional head (aux3), so the existing 4-target head and old nets are untouched. Built from the shards (same game, turn + 10, else the end). Why it might add something over the end-of-game holder: the end holder is mostly decided by the game's result and noise from 50+ turns of dice; a 10-turn target is close in time, so it rewards seeing the plan itself. Arms: control / features / aux10 / both. The probe in step 0 also tells whether round 73 already predicts it.
  4. **Step C, self-play that tries the plans (only if A or B gains):** targeted starts from positions where a 2-3 road join or a 3-road Longest Road swing is available (targeted_starts.py, with these features as the selector). E8's generic tactical starts were null (485-515 of 1,000), so this goes last and needs the features to aim it. A rule opponent that executes road plans is E60's league (phase 1+), not a separate build.
- **Metrics:** (1) gate against round 73, 4,000 games at 200 simulations (52% passes) and an equal-thinking-time match (1,000+ games), because the features cost time; (2) live-loss's failure-set road cases (net's move vs the right road move); (3) offline: value error on people's positions at turn 30+ and in the even band, paired by game; (4) live after adoption only: dead roads per game (round 73: 2.06 ± 0.34), Longest Road taken first (59.8%), win rate when the person takes it first (34-48%).
- **Why n-step or TD value targets would not fix it:** they bootstrap from the same net's later values, which also cannot see the plan, so they cut variance but not this bias; E16 already mixed search values in up to weight 1.0 without moving the late error.
- **Cost:** step 0 CPU only. Steps A/B: about one task-day of code, about 1.5 h CPU precompute and 4 h GPU on the PC, then a fleet gate (4,000 games per arm) and an equal-time match. No production change; adoption is the lead's call (and the owner's, since it changes the bots' speed).
- **Status:** plan only. Step 0 is next once E54 is read (it does not need the GPU).
- **Step 0 RESULT (Oct 19, 11:40 PM Toronto): STOP.** scratch/research/e65_probe.py, round 73 raw, turn 20+, mover's view, board decoded from the shards. 5-fold by game; z − v fit on a bias alone vs bias + 12 plan features + features × v.
  - People's holdout (701 games, 20,745 positions): MSE v 0.4574, v+bias 0.4526, v+features 0.4549. Gain of the features over the bias **−0.0023** (per game −0.0013 ± 0.0038). Turn 60+ (569 games, 7,056): −0.0035 (−0.0020 ± 0.0056).
  - Our ranked games (832 shards, 819 games, 24,699 positions): v 0.4316, v+bias 0.4249, v+features 0.4245, gain **+0.0004** (−0.0001 ± 0.0040); turn 60+ +0.0004 (± 0.0047).
  - Probe of the global vector (ridge, R²): longest road now and with 1-3 more roads 0.79-0.90 (the net already knows them); join distance and distance to a free spot about 0 (−0.02 to 0.04), and the 3-road gain 0.04-0.05. So the net does not encode join or spot, but they carry no information about the result beyond its value. The R² rule was met only for numbers that do not matter; the error rule (0.003+) failed on both sets.
  - Side note: a plain bias fit alone lowers the error 0.005 (people) and 0.007 (our games), the known optimism, not a road effect.
  - Caveat: tests the value only, linearly; a policy effect (choosing the joining road) is not tested. Steps A-C not built. Line closed unless live-loss shows road-plan mistakes the policy makes with a clear margin.

## E59 — check: the softer target also moves the value target, slightly (research, Oct 19, 11:45 PM Toronto)
- Production trains with train.py's default value mix 0.5 (the loop never passes --value-mix), so half the value target is the stored search value, and that value is the average of the move values weighted by the recorded move target. A softer target therefore lowers it a little.
- Measured (scratch/research/e59_v.py, round 73 at 400 simulations, 5 games, about 250 positions; the same searches, only the scale changes): scale 0.5 shifts the search value by -0.003 (±0.001), -0.004 at turn 60+; scale 0.25 by -0.006 to -0.009. In the value target at half mix: -0.0015 at 0.5. Negligible next to the net's 10-point bias in even positions, and in the safe direction. Not a blocker. Any later value-mix comparison across the switch should still remember it.

## E66 — research: a people model tuned to the 2300+ players (PROPOSED by the lead, Oct 20, 8:50 AM Toronto; offline first)
- **Hypothesis:** the opponent model (one net on all people we played, about 1800-2500) blurs strong players' style with weak players'. A model fit to 2300+ players (trained on their moves only, or with the opponent's rating band as an input) predicts their moves better and makes the search's opponent model more accurate where the Oct 30 checkpoint counts.
- **Source:** Maia (McIlroy-Young, Sadler, Anderson, Kleinberg, 'Aligning Superhuman AI with Human Behavior: Chess as a Model System', KDD 2020): a human-move model trained on one rating band predicts that band best and falls off for other bands. Pre-game ratings are in the game-over messages (live-play's e61_band.py).
- **Metric:** offline, top-1 accuracy and cross-entropy on held-out 2300+ opponents' moves, tuned vs current (current: 70.3% top-1 on all people's moves). Go live only with at least +1 point on 2300+ moves and at most -1 on the rest; then, after E63's verdict, as the opponent model on the opponent-model account, measured by its 2300+ results.
- **First step:** research counts 2300+ opponent games and moves; under about 150 games, use the band-as-input form or wait. No production change; the hourly people-model refresh is untouched.
- **Oct 20, 8:47 AM (research): count.** Opponent pre-game MMR (rankedUserStates previousMMR) for every opponent_exact shard (scratch/research/e66_count.py; map in data/orchestrator/e66/ratings.json). Train split: 2300+ 149 games / 6,815 moves; 2100-2300 1,343 / 62,484; under 2100 2,662 / 129,413; unrated 75 / 3,668. Holdout: 2300+ 23 / 855; 2100-2300 219 / 10,589; under 2100 484 / 22,918; unrated 6 / 244. Opponent MMR p10/p50/p90 1704 / 2050 / 2217. 172 games in all, just over the 150 bar, but the holdout's 855 moves give about ±3 points on top-1, too coarse for the 1-point bar.
- **Step 2 design (ran 8:49-9:15 AM):** 4-fold cross-check by game over all 172 2300+ games (fold = game id mod 4; scratch/research/e66_cv.py, PC GPU under gpu.lock tag e66-fold-K). Per fold: control = opponent_model train (production's recipe, 10 epochs) from runs/g1/best.npz (round 150) on the train split minus that fold's 2300+ games (other folds' 2300+ games included, holdout ones too); tuned = control fine-tuned on the other folds' 2300+ games only, lr 1e-4, 10 epochs, best validation epoch. Scored per game on the fold's 2300+ moves (about 1,900 a fold, 7,670 pooled) and on the holdout's 33,751 other moves; 95% intervals by game bootstrap on the tuned-minus-control difference. Verdict: tuned at least +1 point top-1 on pooled 2300+ with the interval above 0, and no worse than -1 on the rest; else try the band-as-input form (an architecture change) or close. Cost: about 45 min of PC GPU, no money. Results in data/orchestrator/e66/cv.txt.
- **Oct 20, 9:15 AM (research): RESULT, no gain; the separate-model form fails the bar.** Ran 8:49-9:15 AM (4 folds, about 6 min each on the PC's GPU; gpu.lock released). Pooled 2300+ (7,670 moves, 172 games): top-1 control 71.24%, tuned 71.20%, diff -0.04 points (95% by game -0.50 to +0.43); CE 0.7961 vs 0.7949 (diff -0.0012, -0.0069 to +0.0044). Per fold: -0.78, +0.45, 0.00, -0.06 points. Rest of the holdout (33,751 moves, 709 games, per fold): tuned -0.28 to -0.54 points, CE +0.011 to +0.018, all intervals below 0. So +1 point is outside the interval: fine-tuning on 2300+ moves only adds nothing they don't already share with everyone, and costs the rest. Side finding: the general model already predicts 2300+ players better (71.2%) than the rest (70.4%), so strong players are not a different style the general model blurs, at least at this data size. Band as an input: not built; with fine-tuning flat at +0.0 ± 0.5, a rating input has no visible signal to pick up. Recommendation: close E66 for now; re-run e66_count.py and e66_cv.py when we hold about 400 2300+ games (our accounts now sit at 2400+, so most new opponents will be 2300+). Nothing changed in production.

## E67 — training: the gate's extra 4,000 games only from 50.4% (APPROVED by the lead, Oct 20, 2:50 PM Toronto)
- **What:** the promotion match plays 4,000 games and, on a near miss, 4,000 more. The extension now happens only when the first 4,000 score 50.4% or more; below that the round is a plain keep (training's --extend-below 0.504 in train.sh). Training lands it and restarts the loop with safe_restart_training.sh at a safe point.
- **Why:** efficiency's estimate (Oct 20, 12:16 PM): the gate is about two thirds of the fleet's compute (8,000 games at 200 searches against 5,000 self-play games at the 400/24 mix). Training's replay over rounds 96-155 (scratch/training/gate_stops.py): about 14% of gate games saved, no promotion lost (round 152 passed with 50.6% after 4,000; round 158 at 50.2% would have been kept at 4,000 and was kept at 8,000 anyway). An early stop at 2,000 games would have lost round 152, so that is not taken.
- **Metric:** gate games per round and minutes per round over the next 10 rounds (today: gate 31-40 min, train 16); each near miss checked against the old rule.
- **Revert:** drop the flag from train.sh and restart at a safe point.
- **Training's numbers (replay of rounds 96-155, 60 rounds, 346,000 gate games, 5,767 a round, 2 promotions: 150 and 152):** extending only from 50.2% / 50.4% / 50.5% / 50.6% would have skipped 6 / 12 / 15 / 16 extensions, 7% / 14% / 17% / 18% of gate games, with no lost promotion (round 150 was 51.9% at 4,000, round 152 about 50.6%). 50.4% leaves room under round 152. Other stops looked at and not taken: a stop at 2,000 games under 49.0% saves 8%, and at 49.5% or higher it loses round 152 (49.0% at 2,000, 51.0% at the end); a stop at 1,000 games under 51% saves only 2%, under 51.5% saves 12% but loses round 152.
- **Deployed:** landed Oct 20, 2:55 PM Toronto (train.sh --extend-below 0.504, was 0.50). The loop restarts by safe_restart_training.sh when round 160's match begins, so round 160 is the first round under the new rule; round 159 (51.0% at 4,000, extending) is on the old rule. Details: commit 57144d5.
- **Result (training, Oct 20, 11:45 PM Toronto): KEPT, but it saved nothing in its 10 rounds.** Rounds 160-169 scored 51.2, 50.6, 49.65, 50.9, 50.6, 49.80, 50.6, 49.15, 50.5 and 50.8% after 4,000 games. None fell in the 50.0-50.4% band where the new rule stops and the old one extends, so both rules played the same 68,000 gate games (6,800 a round: 7 extended, 3 stopped at 4,000). No promotion was lost: round 168 passed at 51.12% over 8,000 after 50.5% at 4,000, just above the line (a 50.6% line would have lost it, so the line should not go higher). Gate time: 16-20 min for a 4,000-game match, 32-46 min for 8,000; a round takes about an hour. The near misses have grown more common because the new nets sit close to the best (round 96-155: 5,767 gate games a round). The rule stays; it costs nothing. Details: train.log lines 12128-13021.
- **Tally, rounds 160-183 (training, Oct 21, 8:55 AM Toronto; the lead's request).** 24 gates, 137,000 games (5,708 a round). 12 stopped at 4,000 (162, 165, 167, 171-174, 176-178, 180, 183), 11 went to 8,000 (160, 161, 163, 164, 166, 168, 169, 175, 179, 181, 182), and round 170 stopped at 1,000 on the futility rule (47.4%). **The rule saved 5 extensions, all in rounds 172-180**, which scored 50.05-50.30% at 4,000 (172, 174, 176, 177, 180): 20,000 games, about 13% of the gate games the old rule would have played, and about 82 minutes of gate time (an extension takes 16-17 minutes). To pass (51% over 8,000) those five would have needed 51.7-52.0% in the next 4,000, more than 2 standard errors above what they had shown; the chance is about 1-3% each, so most likely none was a lost promotion. **The near misses that were extended (50.4% or more at 4,000)**: 160 (51.1 to 50.80), 161 (50.6 to 50.82), 163 (50.8 to 50.42), 164 (50.6 to 50.52), 166 (50.6 to 50.40), 169 (50.8 to 50.09), 175 (51.7 to 50.58), 179 (51.0 to 50.22), 181 (50.5 to 50.40) were kept; **168 (50.5 to 51.12) and 182 (51.0 to 51.29) passed**. Both promotions came from above the line (round 168 by 0.1 point), so it should not go higher; a 50.6% line would have lost round 168. The new rule played 117,000 games where the old one would have played 137,000. Details: scratch/training/gate_stops.py 160; train.log to line 14086.

## E68 — training: a people-like opponent as a yardstick for new nets (the lead's research item, Oct 20, 2:50 PM Toronto; APPROVED, measurement first)
- **Hypothesis:** nets that gain in self-play but not live do worse than round 73 against an opponent that plays like people. The people model (models/human/net.npz, the net Vasos5306 plays with our search at 58.9% live) is a cheap stand-in for such a player.
- **Source:** our own data: live, rounds 150/152 are 22-24 against round 73's 59% and 9-19 against 2100-2300 players (Oct 20, 2:40 PM); on the people holdout their value error is 0.010 worse at 2100+ (training, 12:05 PM). AlphaStar (Vinyals et al., Nature 2019) judged agents against a league of different players, including exploiters, because the latest self is a biased judge.
- **Measurement:** a fixed copy of the people model (snapshot it; the hourly refresh must not move under the match). Round 73 vs people model and round 152 vs people model, 200 searches a move each side, 2,000 games each, on the fleet (about 10 minutes each at today's 4.3 games/s). Same seeds for both.
- **Verdict:** round 152's score minus round 73's (each ±2.2 points). Trails by 3 points or more: the gate gets a people-model arm (a new net must score within 2 points of the best against the people model, else kept), designed by training for the lead's approval. Level: the people model is no better a yardstick than self-play; E69's live reading decides.
- **Result (training, Oct 20, 3:28 PM Toronto): round 152 does NOT trail. No gate arm.** Against a copy of the people model taken at 2:57 PM (it refreshed again at 3:03 PM), 200 searches a move each side, 2,000 games each, same seed: round 73 54.10% ± 2.16 (1082-918), round 152 55.95% ± 2.18 (1119-881). Round 152 is ahead by 1.85 points (the gap's 95% range is about ±3.1, so level to slightly ahead). Like self-play, this yardstick does not see the live gap; E69's live reading decides. Caveat: the people model is refreshed from runs/g1/best.npz (round 152 since 9:59 AM) and then fit to people's moves, so its value head and style are round 152's family. That may make it a biased judge between round 73 and round 152. A fairer people-like opponent would be trained from a fixed seed that is neither net; that is research's area. Details: scratch/training/e68.py, data/orchestrator/e68/results.txt, snapshot sha 84f516847a618134; round 152's match ran beside round 160's gate (1131 s against 732 s).

The only account that could test it live in the band that counts is Dilly6283, which the owner moved back to round 73 today; Tedi4960 (1856) plays players where this net is level with round 152, so a trial there would say nothing. ## E70 — research: does E54's exploiter beat us the way people do? (lead's research item, Oct 20, 8:45 PM Toronto; ORDERED)
- **Hypothesis:** if the exploiter's wins over round 152 look like people's wins over the bot (cities, a road race, long games), training against it (E60 phase 1) attacks the live weakness directly; if it wins some other way, a league would give another self-play gain people do not feel.
- **Source:** AlphaStar's league exploiters (Vinyals et al., Nature 2019) and live-loss's loss_shape.py: in people's wins over the bot at 2100+ the person builds about 3.2 cities and 11.1 roads and the game runs about 81 rolls.
- **Measurement:** E54 step 30 vs round 152, 200 searches a move each, 1,000 games on the fleet at COLONIST_MATCH_NICE=19, each game's record kept (turns, each side's roads, settlements, cities, development cards bought and played, longest road and largest army holders, final VP). Same statistics for round 152's wins over round 152 (the self-play control) and for people's wins at 2100+ (live-loss's numbers).
- **Verdict (the lead):** the exploiter's cities, roads, longest-road share and game length with 95% ranges, against the people's numbers and the self-play control. Within the people's range on cities and roads and longer games than the self-play control: E60 phase 1 is built with this net as the exploiter. Otherwise the league is set aside and research returns to the people gap.
- **Cost:** about 10 minutes of fleet at low priority. **Revert:** nothing live.
- **Result (research, Oct 20, 9:35 PM Toronto; DONE, verdict the lead's):** ran 9:05-9:19 PM at niceness 19. Step 30 vs round 152: 55.8% ± 2.9 (558-442, 1,000 games, seeds 7700000000+, first seat 52.6%). Round 152 vs itself: 49.0% ± 4.2 (500 games, seeds 7710000000+). Pieces on the board at the end (human_dataset's definitions), means ± 95%:

  | wins | n | cities | settlements | roads | rolls | longest road | largest army |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | step 30 over r152 | 558 | 3.37 ± 0.06 | 3.14 ± 0.10 | 12.30 ± 0.18 | 81.2 ± 1.1 | 83% ± 3 | 74% ± 4 |
  | r152 over step 30 | 442 | 3.38 ± 0.07 | 3.21 ± 0.12 | 11.78 ± 0.22 | 80.7 ± 1.4 | 77% ± 4 | 71% ± 4 |
  | r152 over r152 (control) | 500 | 3.43 ± 0.06 | 2.97 ± 0.11 | 11.54 ± 0.20 | 79.7 ± 1.3 | 80% ± 3 | 78% ± 4 |
  | people over the bot, 2100+, all nets | 747 | 3.33 ± 0.05 | 3.50 ± 0.08 | 11.90 ± 0.16 | 82.8 ± 1.0 | not recorded | not recorded |
  | people, 2100+, round 73 | 230 | 3.28 ± 0.10 | 3.49 ± 0.14 | 11.77 ± 0.30 | 82.2 ± 1.9 | | |
  | people, 2100+, round 150+ | 19 | 3.16 ± 0.34 | 3.53 ± 0.69 | 11.11 ± 1.15 | 80.9 ± 5.6 | | |

  Step 30's wins against the control: roads +0.76 ± 0.27, settlements +0.17 ± 0.15, cities -0.06 ± 0.08, rolls +1.5 ± 1.7 (not significant), longest road +3 ± 5 points. Winners' VP 15.05-15.06 in all three; development cards played 8.0-8.3 (knights 5.5-5.8). **Reading:** all three kinds of win have the same shape; these coarse numbers don't single out the exploiter. By the lead's rule: cities within the people's range, roads 0.4 above it, games not longer than the control's, so the rule is not met. The one difference from people in every self-play net is settlements kept on the board (people 3.50, nets 2.97-3.21). Records: data/orchestrator/e70/*.jsonl (diagnostics' new "final" block, landed ce13131); stats: scratch/research/e70_stats.py.
- **Did the low-priority switch protect the loop's gate? (efficiency, Oct 20, 10:05 PM Toronto; one partial reading):** E70 overlapped only the last 4 minutes of round 166's extra 4,000 games (8:51-9:08 PM); the control (9:13-9:19 PM) overlapped no gate. Round 166's 8,000 games averaged 138.7 ms a move; the gates alone before and after were 130.7-131.6 (rounds 163-165, 167). So the extra 4,000 ran at about 146 ms (+11%) and took about 1,035 s against about 950 s for the first 4,000 (+9%). If all of that came from the 4-minute overlap, the gate was about 50% slower while E70 ran, against 100% slower in round 161's overlap without the switch (265 ms). Reading: the switch roughly halves the cost but does not remove it; one overlap, so not a verdict. E70 itself thought 224 ms a move during the overlap and 301 ms in the control with no gate, so side matches are held back mostly by self-play, not by the gate.

## E61 — RERUN on Dilly6283 (bot3) (lead, Oct 20, 8:45 PM Toronto; live-play starts it at the end of the current game)
- **What:** Sherie3424's final E61 settings on Dilly6283: 10 s and 12,000 simulations for a turn's moves (was 5 s, 4,800), 2 s for the roll when a card can be played first, robber share 0.4 of its 15-second clock. The opening and discards unchanged. E62 step 2 stays on.

Its baseline is already clean: round 73 over its last 300 games 62.1% (164-100; 2100-2300 60.7%, 2300+ 6-9), and 20-9 in the 29 games since today's pin. Cost: about 25% fewer games a day.

## E74 — training: 6,000 self-play games a round instead of 5,000 (training's call, Oct 21, 12:05 AM Toronto, on efficiency's finding of Oct 20, 11:56 PM)
- **What:** SELFPLAY_GAMES=6000 in workers.env (was 5000). Window (18 rounds) and sample (12M positions) unchanged, so training time is unchanged: round 170 already trained on the 12M cap (11.40M train + 0.60M validation from 125,003 games in 33 folders).
- **Why:** efficiency's idle_gap.py over rounds 160-170: self-play's 5,000 games take 11.2-11.9 min in a normal round while training takes 15.2-16.1, so the fleet idles 2.6-4.2 min; 6,113-6,866 games would have fit in 8 of those rounds (162-166, 168-170). Rounds 160, 161 and 167 ran long (14.8-24.1 min of self-play, fleet losses) and had no idle time; there a 6,000-game round makes the gate share CPUs for a few minutes (as in round 161). About 20% more fresh games an hour; the plateau plan's lever is more distinct data.
- **Metric:** (1) mechanics, rounds 173-175: self-play ends before training ends, round length unchanged; (2) gate over 10 rounds against rounds 160-169 (49.15-51.2% at 4,000, one promotion: round 168) and late live error (0.383 at round 169).
- **Deployed:** workers.env edited Oct 21, 12:05 AM Toronto; safe_restart_training.sh restarts the loop when round 171's match begins and round 172's early self-play is done, so round 173 is the first 6,000-game round.
- **Revert:** SELFPLAY_GAMES=5000 in workers.env and safe_restart_training.sh.
- **LIVE (training, Oct 21, 3:05 AM Toronto):** the loop restarted at 12:15 AM; round 172's self-play was already the first at 6,000 (18 machines, done in time). Mechanics pass (efficiency, rounds 173-175): 6,000 games take 12.2-12.6 min and end about 3 min before training; the gate is not delayed; about 6,100-6,200 would fit, so no room to go higher. Gates so far at 4,000: r172 50.30%, r173 49.43%, r174 50.18%, r175 51.7% (near miss, extended to 8,000). Late live error 0.388/0.390/0.392 at r172-174 (0.383 at r169); still within the 0.383-0.392 drift seen while round 168 stays best, watch it. Verdict after round 181.
- **VERDICT: KEPT, no gain shown (training, Oct 21, 7:00 AM Toronto).** Gates r172-181 (vs round 168): 50.30, 49.43, 50.18, 50.58 (8,000), 50.05, 50.22, 49.85 (r178), 50.22 (8,000), 50.10, 50.40 (8,000) %; mean 50.13%, range 49.43-50.58, no promotion. Rounds 160-169 scored 49.15-51.2% with one promotion, so the gate shows no change either way (one promotion in 10 is within chance). Late live error 0.388-0.395 (peak r176), 0.391 at r181, against 0.383 at r169: a drift of under 0.01 while the best net is unchanged, and under the 0.40 flag. Kept because it costs nothing (self-play ends before training, round length unchanged) and adds about 20% more distinct games to the window; it does not by itself break the plateau.
- **Round 182 promoted (Oct 21, 7:47 AM Toronto), 2 rounds after the verdict:** 51.0% at 4,000, 51.29% over 8,000, the first promotion in 14 rounds and the first trained on 6,000-game rounds only. Round 183 kept it (49.50%). On the people holdout (training, 8:55 AM; human2100.py, 243 games at 2100+, 9,562 positions) round 182 is no better than the others: value error 0.620 (round 73 0.610, 152 0.620, 168 0.618), late 0.404 (0.399 / 0.405 / 0.401), round 182 minus round 73 +0.010 (95% -0.003 to +0.023). It matches people's moves a little more often (63.6% top-1, against 62.9 / 63.1 / 63.4%).

## E71 — research: the late game against people, task 3 (Oct 20, 9:30 PM Toronto; step 0 PROPOSED, not built)
- **What is already settled (read before proposing):** E16 (value target mix up to 1.0): no change in late error. E23 (people's games weighted 16x/32x): no. E49: late error by round is flat (0.404-0.423), a single shrink v → c·v gains at most 0.001, the ranking of late positions (AUC 0.940-0.942) is the same for every net, and a 0.01 late-error gap shows no Elo live (round 66 vs 73). E65 step 0: road-plan numbers add nothing to the value. So a loss or target change (target mix, turns-to-the-end head, people-only late value) has nothing measurable to fix; the three candidates in the brief are closed by these results. What remains is that people come back from late leads that self-play opponents do not (E49 step 2: self-play converts late leads as the net says).
- **E70's lead, checked (scratch/research/e71_settle.py, every live game with a converted capture; pieces on the board at the end, means ± 95%):**

  | games | n | person settlements | person cities | person roads | bot settlements | bot cities | bot roads | rolls |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 2100+, bot won | 835 | 3.09 ± 0.09 | 1.55 ± 0.08 | 7.8 ± 0.2 | 2.67 ± 0.09 | 2.68 ± 0.07 | 9.1 ± 0.2 | 66.9 ± 1.4 |
  | 2100+, person won | 747 | 3.50 ± 0.08 | 3.33 ± 0.05 | 11.9 ± 0.2 | 2.85 ± 0.11 | 1.94 ± 0.08 | 9.8 ± 0.2 | 82.8 ± 1.0 |
  | under 2100, bot won | 1,313 | 3.23 ± 0.07 | 1.59 ± 0.07 | 8.3 ± 0.2 | 2.68 ± 0.08 | 2.85 ± 0.06 | 9.6 ± 0.2 | 68.8 ± 1.1 |
  | under 2100, person won | 813 | 3.57 ± 0.08 | 3.42 ± 0.05 | 12.1 ± 0.2 | 2.84 ± 0.11 | 1.95 ± 0.08 | 9.9 ± 0.2 | 82.6 ± 0.9 |

  **Reading:** people keep 0.4-0.7 more settlements than the bot whether they win or lose, in both bands, so it is a style, not a comeback signature. The bot keeps 2.7-2.85 settlements against people in wins and losses alike (self-play winners 2.97-3.21, E70). People's wins are long (83 rolls) and the bot's wins are short (67-69): when the game goes long, people win it. This fits "people come back late" and says nothing new about the loss.
- **Proposal, step 0: a yardstick that sees the late gap (CPU only, no training).** Every offline test so far starts from an empty board (gate, E68's people model), and none sees the live gap. Start from people's own late positions instead: cuts at turns 50-90 from the people holdout (starts.py's cut and replay, --min-turn 50 --max-turn 90, holdout split; a small scratch filter, no code landed), the bot's chair played by the net under test, the person's chair by a fixed snapshot of the people model, 200 searches a move each.
  - **Check of the yardstick:** round 73 from 2,000 such starts. Its win rate there vs (a) what happened in the live games from the same positions and (b) round 73 vs round 73 from the same starts. If people model ≈ live (within ±3 points) and self-play converts at least 5 points more, this setup reproduces people's late comebacks offline.
  - **Then the comparison it was built for:** rounds 73 and 152 and E54 step 30 from the same 2,000 starts (paired by start). Live, round 73 beats rounds 150/152 against 2100-2300 players (E69: -11 points, 95% -24.5 to +2.5) and E54 is the most hopeful when behind (E54 verdict). If the yardstick puts round 73 ahead by 3+ points (paired ± about 2), it is the first offline test that agrees with live play, and E50's training (late starts with a people-like seat) has a metric that matters. If not, the late-start line closes and the gap is not in the late game.
  - **Cost:** about 4 × 2,000 games at 200 searches on the coordinator at niceness 19 (gate.py's pace: about 30 min per 3,000 games, so about 2 h of foreground runs over 3-4 tasks), or about 40 min of fleet if the lead gives it. No money, no GPU, nothing live. **Revert:** nothing deployed.
  - **Built (Oct 20, 9:50 PM Toronto).** Landed f6aae69: an evaluate task can carry a start (starts.pack, the log up to one cut, gzip+base64, about 2 KB) and the color A sits in; tasks without one play as before. scratch/research/e71_starts.py wrote e71/starts.jsonl: 2,085 starts (up to 3 cuts a game, seeded by the game) from 699 of the 762 holdout games, 550 of them with the person's rating. scratch/research/e71_late.py NAME A B [--limit N] [--local W] plays one game a start, seed 7,100,000,000 + start index, A in the bot's chair, people model = E68's snapshot (data/orchestrator/e68/people_net.npz, sha 84f516847a618134), and prints the net's and live win rates by band. Smoke: 16 starts locally, 191 s on 8 processes at niceness 19 (about 7 h an arm here), so the arms wait for fleet time from the lead (4 × 2,085 games, about 10 min each at niceness 19).
  - **Caveats:** the people model is fit from round 152's net (E68's caveat), so it may favour round 152; the check against live comebacks above catches that. Live games from a given late position are one sample each, so (a) is a game-level comparison over about 700 holdout games.

## E72 — study: the "cut-off" second settlement, and the seat split (live-play, Oct 20, 10:57 PM Toronto; owner's question of 10:52 PM via the lead; STUDY, nothing live)
- **Data:** every live game in data/human/index.jsonl with a converted capture, all accounts, Oct 11-21: 5,215 games (5,158 ranked; 99 with a 2300+ opponent rating in live-loss's cache, which only covers Oct 18+). The records do carry the placement order (setup: seat 1 settles, seat 2 settles twice, seat 1 settles). Scripts: scratch/live-play/cutoff_study.py (rows in cutoff_rows.jsonl) and cutoff_report.py. Only games played by people with us; no outside 2300+ dataset exists in capture form, so "top players" = our own 2300+ opponents (about 50 a seat, too few for anything but the seat split).
- **Definitions (for X's second settlement n, opponent Y):** *wood+brick* = n touches a wood and a brick hex. *Y's target T* = among free legal nodes 2-3 steps from Y's FIRST settlement, the one round 152's net puts the highest settlement prior on from Y's chair (position just before X's placement). *Cut* = n is T, on a shortest path from Y's first settlement to T, or next to T (kills it by the distance rule); *strict* drops "next to T". *Cut-off trick* = cut and wood+brick. "Available" = some legal node was a cut-off spot.
- **Seat split (bot's seat, all games):** seat 1 61.0% [59.1-62.8] (1,569/2,573), seat 2 54.6% [52.7-56.5] (1,442/2,642): a 6.4-point gap. Against 2300+: 57.1% (28/49) vs 42.0% (21/50); under 2100: 66.6% vs 57.3%.
- **Q1, do people do it to us?** Wood+brick second settlement: people 37.1% as seat 1 and 57.1% as seat 2; our bot 21.6% and 24.3%. Cut-off trick: people 9.5% (seat 1) and 16.4% (seat 2); when a spot was available they took it 25.3% [22.6-28.1] and 33.4% [30.9-36.1]. 2300+ people: 4% (2/50) and 18% (9/49), no sign they do it more. Our win rate when they do it: opponent in seat 1 (so we are seat 2) 48.8% [42.7-55.0] (122/250) vs 55.2% [53.2-57.2] (1,320/2,392) when not, about 6 points, under 2 sigma; opponent in seat 2: 61.4% vs 60.9%, no difference.
- **Q2, does our bot do it?** Cut-off trick 6.1% (seat 1) and 8.3% (seat 2); taken when available 21.2% and 20.4%, below people's 25-33%. By net (spot taken when available, seat 1 / seat 2): round 73 17.5% (24/137) / 21.1% (45/213); people model 18% / 20% (22/35 games); round 152, E54, round 150 under 20 games a seat. Our win rate when we do it: seat 2 59.5% [52.9-65.8] (131/220) vs 54.1% (1,311/2,422), +5 points under 2 sigma; seat 1 59.5% vs 61.1%, none.
- **Counterfactual (prior vs search):** when a cut-off spot was available, the playing net's mean prior on those spots was 0.19 (seat 1) and 0.25 (seat 2); it was the prior's top spot 20-26% of the time. The search took it 74-84% of the times it was the prior's top and 2-3% of the times it was not; overall the search picks the prior's top settlement 84% of the time. So the choice is the prior's, and search does not discover the trick.
- **Wood+brick alone does not predict the result:** our win rate is the same whether either side's second settlement is wood+brick (e.g. bot seat 2: 53.4% vs 54.9%; opponent seat 2: 62.2% vs 59.4%).
- **Reading:** people do favour wood+brick second settlements (about 2x as often as we do), and they cut our corridor somewhat more often, but neither is a big lever on its own: the only hints are about 6 points in our seat-2 games (hurt when the seat-1 person cuts us, helped when we cut them), each under 2 sigma and confounded by the board (a cut-off spot that is also good is taken by everyone). The seat-2 gap (6.4 points) is not explained by this. Caveats: T comes from one net's prior from a chair it does not sit in; "corridor" is 2-3 steps from the first settlement only.
- **Next (proposed, opening study part 2):** the seat-2 gap: compare our seat-2 opening (both settlements' pips, resource mix, ports) with people's seat-2 openings that win, and check the seat-2 split in self-play by net. A live test of a wood+brick bias for seat 2 only if part 2 points to it. **Revert:** nothing deployed.
- **Part 2 (Oct 20, 11:25 PM Toronto): the seat-2 gap is the game's first-move edge, not a weak seat-2 opening.** Same 5,215 games; scratch/live-play/open_study.py (rows in open_rows.jsonl; `open_study.py report` reprints). Pips of both setup settlements (6 − |7 − number|), by resource. Our seat-2 opening is as rich as the seat-1 person's: 21.42 pips vs 21.26 (2100+: 21.40 vs 21.26), and as rich as our own seat-1 opening (21.72). Our openings in both seats lean ore+wheat (10.8 / 10.0 pips vs people's 8.2-8.9) and away from wood+brick (7.1 / 7.5 vs 8.3-8.7). People's seat-2 openings in games they won against us: 21.32 pips, wood+brick 8.48, ore+wheat 8.48, no richer than ours. Within our seat-2 games the pip edge over the person tracks the result (under −3: 39%, −3 to 0: 51%, 0 to 3: 57%, over 3: 60%) and more ore+wheat goes with more wins (10+ pips 57.7% vs 4-7 pips 47.6%), but no wood+brick band does better (52-57%, 2100+: 42-57% on 64-177 games, noisy). The gap itself is about ±3.2 points around 57.8%, i.e. the first seat is worth about 6 points to whoever holds it; self-play gives seat 1 54.5-57.8% (E18), the same size. **Verdict: no seat-2 opening change is indicated; no live test.** Caveat: the port column reads 2-3% for both sides, which looks like ports are not mapped in converted records; ports are not judged here. The self-play split by net was not redone (E18 covers it).
- **Recheck (live-loss, Oct 21, 10:30 PM Toronto; owner's question of 9:43 PM via the lead): the gap is real and even across bands, but self-play at live search does NOT show a 6-point first-move edge, so "it is the first-move edge" is unproven.** Scripts: scratch/live-loss/seat_band.py (ratings from each capture's game-over message, now cached for all of open_rows.jsonl, Oct 11 on) and seat_selfplay.py.
  - **(1) Live, by opponent rating at game time** (5,048 of 5,158 ranked games have both ratings; Wilson 95%):

    | opponent | bot seat 1 | bot seat 2 | gap | person seat 1 | person seat 2 |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | under 2100 | 62.8% [60.4, 65.1] (1,619) | 56.9% [54.4, 59.2] (1,641) | +6.0 ± 3.4 | 43.1% | 37.2% |
    | 2100-2300 | 56.6% [53.1, 60.0] (776) | 48.8% [45.4, 52.2] (838) | +7.8 ± 4.9 | 51.2% | 43.4% |
    | 2300+ | 48.4% [38.5, 58.4] (93) | 42.0% [31.8, 52.8] (81) | +6.4 ± 14.8 | 58.0% | 51.6% |
    | all | 60.3% [58.4, 62.2] (2,488) | 53.8% [51.8, 55.7] (2,560) | +6.6 ± 2.7 | 46.2% | 39.7% |

    The person's seat-1 edge is the same number by construction in 1v1 (person seat 1 = bot seat 2), so it is a consistency check, not separate evidence: seat 1 helps whoever holds it by the same 6-8 points in every band. Over all games seat 1 wins 53.2% ± 1.4. Seats are assigned evenly (bot seat 1 in 48-53% of games in each band), and a fit of win on rating gap plus seat gives seat 1 +6.6 points at equal ratings (±2.7). Note the account ratings barely predict results here (slope 0.11 of Elo's), so "expected" columns are not useful.
  - **(2) Self-play reference, current family:** gate matches rounds 180-199 (new vs best, 200 sims, fresh boards, no people starts): first seat 51.5% (± 0.3 on the logged totals; rounds 190-199 range 49.0-51.9%), i.e. a seat gap of about 3 points. At live search: E80's 600 games of round 182 vs itself at 2,400 sims (data/orchestrator/e80/games.jsonl, seats balanced): first seat 50.3% ± 4.0, a gap of 0.7 ± 8. E18's 54.5-57.8% (9-16 point gap) was older nets at lower budgets and no longer holds. Live first seat 53.2% ± 1.4 vs self-play at 2,400 sims 50.3% ± 4.0: +2.9 ± 4.2 (not significant); vs the gate's 51.5%: +1.7 ± 1.4 (borderline).
  - **(3) 2300+:** with ratings now read for all captures it is 174 games and the gap is +6.4 ± 14.8, against +6.7 ± 2.8 below 2300: difference −0.2 ± 15.0. The 15-point figure on 99 games (Oct 18+ only) was also well inside its own ±19-point range. Nothing special about the top band.
  - **Outside figures:** none for people's 1v1 Catan. colonist.io publishes no seat statistics (a players' feature request for "win % by placement position" is open: colonist.featureupvote.com/suggestions/84257). A fan tally on a Board Game Arena forum gives player 1 51.7% over 29,618 two-player games (2022), but the thread is unverified and not clearly Catan; a publisher's second-hand claim from tournament data says first placement is no advantage in 4-player games. Neither is usable as a reference.
  - **Reading:** seat 1 is worth about 6.6 points live in every band, but our own nets at live search give it only about 0-3. So the live gap is either a first-move edge that is larger against people than between equal nets, or part of it is the bot playing seat 2 worse against people (E72 part 2 found no weak seat-2 opening by pips, so any such weakness would be later). A cheap test, if wanted: the people model (models/human/net.npz or e81/people_net.npz) vs round 189 at 2,400 sims, 1,000 games, read the bot's seat split; if it is ~6 points there too, it is people-like play that gains from moving first. **Revert:** nothing deployed.

## E70 — VERDICT: the exploiter wins like self-play, not like people; the league (E60) is set aside (lead, Oct 21, 8:50 AM Toronto)
- The rule (cities and roads within people's range, games longer than the self-play control) is not met: E54's wins over round 152 look like round 152's own wins (cities 3.37 vs 3.43, roads 12.3 vs 11.5, 81.2 vs 79.7 rolls, longest road 83% vs 80%); roads are 0.4 above people's 11.9 and the games are no longer than the control's.
- What does single people out, in E70 and E71's tables: settlements kept on the board (people 3.50, every self-play net 2.97-3.21, the bot against people 2.7-2.85) and long games (people's wins 83 rolls, the bot's wins 67-69). That lead goes to live-loss as E75.
- E60: set aside; E54's net stays on disk. The league returns only if an offline yardstick that agrees with live play (E71) shows its gain carrying over.

Verdict by E71's rule. Results to the lead by the 3 PM run.

  | arm | all (2,085) | 2100+ (597) | under 2100 (883) | same result as live |
  |---|---|---|---|---|

  | round 73 vs people model | 55.0% ± 2.1 | 49.7% ± 4.0 | 58.0% ± 3.3 | 86.8% |
  | round 73 vs round 73 | 54.3% ± 2.1 | 47.7% ± 4.0 | 58.3% ± 3.3 | 85.4% |
  | round 152 vs people model | 55.3% ± 2.1 | 49.6% ± 4.0 | 58.7% ± 3.2 | 85.8% |

  Paired by start: round 73 minus round 152 −0.3 ± 1.3 points (2100+: +0.2 ± 2.5); round 73 minus E54 −2.0 ± 1.4 (2100+: −0.2 ± 2.5); round 73 vs itself minus round 73 vs people −0.7 ± 1.5 (2100+: −2.0 ± 2.8).
  - **Check (a), people model ≈ live: passed** (55.0% vs 54.5%; 2100+ 49.7% vs 45.7%, about +4 ± 3 paired, the only hint of a late comeback edge for strong people, and every arm shows it alike).
  - **Check (b), self-play converts 5+ points more: failed.** Round 73 against itself wins 54.3%, no more than against the people model. From these positions a self-play opponent comes back as often as people do.

  - **Reading:** the live gap is not in the late game. Whatever makes people beat the bot (or round 73 beat the newer nets live, E69: −11 points, range −24.5 to +2.5, itself not significant) is decided before turn 50. E50's late-start training has no metric here and closes with this line. Script: scratch/research/e71_pair.py (paired numbers); games in data/orchestrator/e71/*.jsonl, lines in e71/results.txt. **Revert:** nothing deployed.

## E75 — live-loss: does the robber take more from the bot than from the person in long games? (lead's research item, Oct 21, 8:50 AM Toronto; ORDERED, study, nothing live)
- **Hypothesis:** the bot builds narrow and city-heavy (fewer settlements than people in every outcome, E71; ore+wheat openings, E72 part 2); a narrow position earns more a roll but loses more to one robber and to every 7, and in 1v1 the opponent's every 7 robs you. If the bot's income is more concentrated and more often blocked than the person's, that is a mechanism for losing the long games (people's wins 83 rolls, the bot's 67), and one self-play opponents do not punish because they build the same way.
- **Source:** E70 and E71's tables (data/orchestrator/e70, scratch/research/e71_settle.py); Catan 1v1 strategy writing on expansion against the robber.
- **Measurement:** over the 2100+ games with converted captures (about 1,582), at every roll from turn 20: each side's expected income a roll (pips, cities doubled), the share blocked by the robber, the share on its single best hex, distinct hexes drawn from, and how often each side's robber sits on the other's best hex. By outcome and game length (under 70 rolls, 70-90, 90+), bot vs person, 95% ranges.
- **Verdict:** the bot's blocked share 10% or more above the person's (relative) in 90+ roll losses, range excluding zero: the mechanism is real; research gets a self-play check (the net's value error on positions where its best hex is robbed) and live-play a robber-move check. No gap: the settlement count is style, the lead looks elsewhere.
- **Cost:** about an hour of coordinator CPU. **Revert:** nothing deployed.
- **RUNNING (live-loss, Oct 21, 8:55 AM Toronto):** replay of 1,621 games vs 2100+ with converted captures, 3 workers at nice 19 (scratch/live-loss/e75_robber.py; per-game sums to data/orchestrator/live-loss/e75_games.json; `e75_robber.py table` reprints). Income in pips a roll (cities doubled); blocked share and the bot-vs-person relative gap are ratios of sums with game-clustered bootstrap ranges; "its robber on opp best" counts rolls where that side placed the robber last.
- **RESULT (live-loss, Oct 21, 9:20 AM Toronto): 1,621 games vs 2100+ replayed, none skipped; 91,532 rolls from turn 20.**
  - Raw table, 90+ roll losses (239 games): the bot's blocked share is 10.2% vs the person's 7.0% in the same games, +45.6% [+33.9, +60.3]. By the letter of the rule, the mechanism is real. But this comparison is mostly outcome: in every length, whoever loses is the one more robbed (bot wins 90+: bot 8.3% vs person 9.1%; bot wins under 70: 6.2% vs 10.9%).
  - Like with like (the bot when it lost vs the person when the person lost, separate games, bootstrap 95%): blocked share +12.0% [+4.0, +21.7] in 90+ games (10.2% vs 9.1%), +9.8% [+4.0, +16.2] at 70-90, +7.4% [+3.1, +11.7] over all lengths; on the winning side +13.2% [+7.5, +19.6]. So the bot is robbed somewhat more than a person in the same position: real, but small. It is about 0.5-0.6 pips a roll, about 1 card a game.
  - Not concentration: on the losing side, the best hex's share of income is equal (+0.6% [-2.9, +4.8] at 90+; 22.7% vs 22.5%) and distinct hexes are equal (8.61 vs 8.68). On the winning side the bot is slightly narrower (best hex +3.6% [+2.0, +5.2], hexes -4.4%). The narrow-build story does not explain the losses.
  - Robber aim is the clearer gap: after the bot moved the robber, it sits on the other side's best hex less often than after a person moved it, in every role and length: losing side 30.5% vs 36.0% (-15.4% [-22.3, -7.3]), winning side 34.4% vs 39.2% (-12.3% [-18.9, -5.3]). The person's robber finds the bot's best hex more than the bot's robber finds the person's.
  - Reading: E75 passes its rule (+12% like with like, the range excludes zero), but the cause is robber aim, not building narrow. Live-play's robber-move check is the follow-up that fits; research's self-play value check matters less. Caveat: "best hex" is by pips only; the bot may aim at a resource it needs, or away from its own buildings (scratch/live-loss/robber.py measured the bot's robber choices earlier). Details: scratch/live-loss/e75_robber.py; `e75_robber.py table` reprints from data/orchestrator/live-loss/e75_games.json.
- **FOLLOW-UP, robber aim against the legal choices (live-loss, Oct 21, 9:35 AM Toronto): same 1,621 games, 32,296 robber moves (bot 16,980, person 15,316); game-clustered bootstrap 95%.** At each move, every legal hex scored three ways, each minus the mover's own pips there: net (opponent pips), dep (opponent pips times that hex's share of the opponent's income of its resource, 1.0 = its only source), scarce (opponent pips weighted by the resource's rarity on the board).
  - Took the best legal hex by net: bot 48.3% vs person 60.7%, gap -12.5 points [-14.0, -10.7]. By dep -10.6 [-12.1, -8.9]; by scarce -13.4 [-15.1, -11.6]. The gap is not explained by resource dependence or scarcity: people aim better on every score.
  - Share of the best reached (net): 71.3% vs 84.6%, -13.3 [-14.3, -12.1].
  - Self-block: the bot puts the robber on its own pips while a hex with the opponent's pips and none of its own exists 15.5% of moves vs 5.2% for people, +10.4 [+9.2, +11.5]; own pips blocked a move 0.90 vs 0.26.
  - Everywhere: turns under 30 -16.5 points (best net), 30-60 -14.2, 60+ -7.7; bot lost -16.4, bot won -8.4; 90+ roll games -14.1, self-block 18.3% vs 5.4%. Worst cell: bot lost, turns 30-60, 36.4% vs 56.4% (-20.0 [-23.3, -16.8]), self-block 19.2% vs 4.7%.
  - Caveats: the scores count pips only, not the steal (in 1v1 every hex touching the opponent steals the same), knights held, or the longest-road race. A search that sees the next few rolls might rationally prefer another hex sometimes, but not on its own pips 3 times as often as people.
  - Reading: the bot's robber placement is measurably worse than people's at 2100+, about 13 points of best-hex choices, and it robs itself 3x as often. Goes to training (the search/prior on MOVE_ROBBER) and live-play (a robber-move check). Next for live-loss: a failure set of the clearest cases (bot on its own pips with a strictly better clean hex) for both to measure against. Script: scratch/live-loss/e75_aim.py; per-move rows in data/orchestrator/live-loss/e75_aim.json; `e75_aim.py table` reprints. **Revert:** nothing deployed.
- **ROBBER FAILURE SET (live-loss, Oct 21, 9:50 AM Toronto).** data/orchestrator/live-loss/robber_set.jsonl: 100 bot robber positions from 100 of the 2100+ games (50 lost; median turn 44), each where the live bot put the robber on its own pips (mean 5.4) while a legal hex had 0 own pips and at least as many opponent pips. Candidates: 1,349 (at most 2 a game) in 843 of 1,624 games; one per game picked with a fixed seed. Each row lists "good" (0 own pips and the best net score) and "bad" (needless self-blocks) hexes. Score any net: `.venv/bin/python data/orchestrator/scratch/live-loss/robber_set.py score NET SIMS [WORKERS]` (SIMS 0 = raw prior). Metric: share good (higher better), share bad (lower better), game-clustered 95%.
  - Round 73, raw prior: good 30% [22, 40], bad 46% [36, 56]. Best net (round 182), raw prior: good 28% [19, 37], bad 43% [33, 53]. Best net, search 200 sims, no noise: good 24% [16, 32], bad 50% [40, 60].
  - The search's own Q, best bad hex minus best good hex (both visited in all 100): median +0.021; bad ahead by more than 0.05 in 24, good ahead by more than 0.05 in 7. The search does not fix the self-block; its values slightly favour it. Caveat: the positions were chosen because the live bot erred there, so they lean toward positions the net misjudges; the scores are a yardstick for change between nets and guards, not a rate for all robber moves.
  - For E78: the guard would change about half these positions at 200 sims. The 24 positions where the search rates the self-block more than 0.05 higher are the ones to look at before deploying.
- **WHY THE SEARCH PREFERS THE SELF-BLOCK (live-loss, Oct 21, 9:45 AM Toronto).** `.venv/bin/python data/orchestrator/scratch/live-loss/robber_dump.py run runs/g1/best.npz 200 3` (same seeds; reproduces 24 good / 50 bad picks, 24 bad-ahead / 69 near tie / 7 good-ahead), rows in data/orchestrator/live-loss/robber_dump.jsonl, `table` reprints. No hidden reason found: the 24 bad-ahead positions look like the rest. Bot VP 6.9 vs 7.1 (near tie) vs 6.3 (good ahead); bot at 8+ VP 46% vs 42% vs 29%; knight move 54/54/43%; knights held 0.2/0.3/0.0; opponent hand 4.4/5.3/6.0. The best clean hex blocks 9.5 opponent pips against the self-block's 5.3 (plus 5.8 of ours), and it holds the larger share of the opponent's income in its resource (70% vs 56%), so even a dependence-weighted score favours it. Resource pairs are spread (wheat-over-wood 4, brick-over-wood 3, the rest 1-2).
  - The search barely separates the hexes: |Q bad - Q good| median 0.030 (90th percentile 0.112), with 24-33 visits each. A position has 1.2 good and 8.3 bad hexes of 18; a uniform pick would be good 7%, bad 46%. The prior picks good 28% (4 times uniform) but bad 43% (as uniform): the net knows the best clean hex a little and does not know the self-block is a cost.
  - Reading: the value net is close to blind to robber placement (a few percent of win chance between hexes), so the choice among the bad hexes is noise. A root guard (E78) loses nothing measurable here; the cure in training would be a sharper value for robber-blocked income, which self-play has little pressure to learn.

## Opening clock — PROPOSAL, not live (live-play, Oct 21, 8:55 AM Toronto; the lead decides at 3 PM)
- **Measured (open_clock.py, last 40 games per account, bridge.log):** every account's two setup settlements hit the 20 s time cap (98-100% of the time) and almost never the 20,000-simulation cap (0-2%). The 20 s comes from BUDGETS (share 0.4 of 57.5 s = 23 s, capped at 20 s), on every VM, not just crowded ones. Colonist allows 60 s.

  | account (VM) | settlement 1: sims (p10-p90), sims/s | settlement 2: sims, sims/s | setup roads (5 s) |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Dilly6283 bot3 (2) | 10,976 (9,144-13,388), 539 | 12,722, 621 | 3,503 |
  | Finlay3621 bot10 (2) | 10,024 (7,944-12,436), 487 | 12,080, 593 | 3,234 |
  | Tedi4960 bot9 (1) | 11,644 (8,944-14,628), 562 | 13,790, 673 | 3,758 |
  | fymouushii bot11 (1) | 12,056 (9,440-14,472), 591 | 14,034, 680 | 3,718 |
  | Vasos5306 bot8 (3) | 8,652 (6,216-12,348), 417 | 9,984, 488 | 2,187 |
  | wifedat bot12 (3) | 8,276 (5,864-10,780), 404 | 9,480, 462 | 2,091 |

  Alone on a VM (before Oct 20, 8:55 PM) the first settlement got 19,000-20,000. Seconds needed now for about 19,500: 29-36 s on VMs 1 and 2, 40-48 s on VM 3.
- **Proposal:** THINK_BUDGETS=BUILD_INITIAL_SETTLEMENT=0.7:40:8.3333 in the account's env file: up to 40 s (0.7 of 57.5 s), simulation cap unchanged at 20,000, so a fast search still stops at 20,000. Leaves about 17 s of colonist's 60 s unused (the bridge runs about 0.5 s over its cap). Expected: 20,000 on both settlements for bot3, bot9, bot11, about 19,500-20,000 for bot10, 16,000-18,500 for bot8 and bot12.
- **Cost:** about 20-40 s more per game (bot3 about 28 s, bot9/bot11 about 21-24 s, bot10 about 33 s, bot8/bot12 about 40 s), against a median 12-14 min between ranked games: about 3-5% fewer games a day.
- **What it can show:** this restores the opening's search rather than adding new search; from about 11,000 to 20,000 is less than a doubling, worth under 1-2 points in self-play (E18; more for the first seat). That is too small to see in 300 live games, so the rule is a safety check, not a win-rate verdict: 50 games with no game lost on time in the opening, no stop, games a day within 5% of before; then all accounts.
- **Which account first:** Dilly6283 (bot3): its opening lost the most against being alone (about 20,000 to 11,000), and nothing else runs on it now. If the owner gives Dilly6283 its own VM (bot-vm-crowding), it gets its 20,000 back without this, and the first account should be wifedat (bot12, the slowest at 8,276).
- **Revert:** delete the THINK_BUDGETS line from the env file and safe_restart_account.sh between games.

## E76 — two searches on one 2-vCPU host: do math threads slow them? (efficiency, Oct 21, 8:50 AM Toronto; lead's request; BENCH, nothing live)
- **Question:** each bot VM now runs two accounts on 2 vCPUs and each searches at about half speed (bot3 1,088 to 514 sims/s). Is part of that threads fighting each other, on top of sharing the cores?
- **Already known:** the bridge sets OMP/OPENBLAS/MKL/VECLIB threads to 1 itself (ranked_runner's ONE_THREAD), and the net is numpy, not torch. So the live bridges should already be single-threaded; the bench checks the size of the effect and whether anything is left.
- **Method:** `data/orchestrator/scratch/efficiency/threads_bench.py` with E54's net: 400-simulation searches on 18 fixed positions, 60 s per arm, 1 and 2 processes at once, default threads vs one thread, two repeats.
- **VM:** bench-2core, e2-standard-2 SPOT in us-east1-b (same type as bot-vm-3), boot disk cloned from selfplay-spot-4. About US$0.03/h with the disk. Fleet then 38 of 40 vCPUs. Deleted as soon as the bench ends (within the hour).
- **Rule:** if one thread gives 10% or more sims/s per process over what the bots run now, tell live-play and the lead.
- **Result (Oct 21, 9:05 AM, done; VM deleted 9:03 AM, about 15 min, under 1 US cent):** e2-standard-2 is one physical core with two hyperthreads (AMD EPYC 7B12). Sims/s per process, two repeats of 60 s each:

  | | 1 search alone | 2 searches at once (each) | 2 at once, total |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | default threads | 1,224 / 1,251 | 231-276 | 462 / 537 |
  | 1 thread | 1,537 / 1,535 | 955 / 956 | 1,910 / 1,912 |

  Default threads are 3.5-4x slower per process when two searches share the host: the threads fight. But **the live bots already run one thread**: the service file sets OMP/OPENBLAS to 1 and ranked_runner's bridge_env sets OMP/OPENBLAS/MKL/VECLIB to 1 for the bridge it starts. So there is nothing to apply in the env files. With one thread, a second search costs each about 38% (1,536 to 955: two hyperthreads share one core). Live, bot3 went 1,088 to 514 (-53%); the extra 15 points are most likely the two Chromium browsers and runners on the same core, not threads. Not checked on a live VM (no ssh from the worker shell).
- **Verdict:** no change. The fix for the crowding is a VM per account (or fewer per VM), not a thread setting.

## E77 — training: if the nine trial self-play VMs go (about Oct 24), 3,400 games a round and a 32-round window (training, Oct 21, 9:10 AM Toronto; PROPOSED, waits for the owner's call on the VMs)
- **Trigger:** efficiency (Oct 21, 9:02 AM) suggested deleting the nine trial self-play VMs around Oct 24 so the GPU host (gpu-train-1, also on the trial credit) runs about 7 days longer. Self-play would lose about 43% of its games.
- **Numbers now (round 183):** 6,000 games in 12.2-12.6 min; training 2 epochs × about 300 s on 11.41M + 0.59M positions from 123,149 games (18 rounds); round about 17 min. On 57% of the fleet, 6,000 games would take about 22 min, so rounds get about 6 min longer and the gate runs about 25% less often; 3,400 games fit in about 12.5 min.
- **What:** SELFPLAY_GAMES=3400, TRAIN_WINDOW=32 in workers.env (TRAIN_SAMPLE and TRAIN_MAX_POSITIONS stay 12M, TRAIN_EPOCHS 2). 32 × 3,400 = 109k games, about the 12M cap (about 97 positions a game), so training time is unchanged. With the window left at 18, training would read about 6M positions.
- **Cost of the choice:** the window spans about 9 h of play instead of about 5 h. On the plateau the nets in that span are equally strong (gate 49.4-50.6% for rounds 172-181), and E31/E11b said distinct data is the lever. Check that loading 32 folders does not slow training's start much.
- **Other option:** keep 6,000 games and accept rounds about 6 min longer: the same games an hour and the same 18-round window, but fewer gates a day.
- **Metric:** mechanics in the first 3 rounds (self-play ends before training; round about 17 min; about 12M positions read); then the gate over 10 rounds against rounds 172-181 (average 50.1%) and late live error (0.391-0.397; flag at 0.40).
- **Revert:** SELFPLAY_GAMES=6000, TRAIN_WINDOW=18, then safe_restart_training.sh.
- **Oct 21, 9:15 AM: SCHEDULED.** The owner keeps the nine VMs until about US$40 of credit is left (efficiency, 9:09 AM). Efficiency plans to delete them Oct 24, about 6 AM Toronto, and confirms on Oct 23 if the time moves. Training sets 3,400 games and the 32-round window in workers.env and runs safe_restart_training.sh right after the VMs leave workers.env, so the first round without them already uses it. Fleet without them: about 2.1 games a second (efficiency), so 3,400 games take about 27 min. Self-play runs alongside the previous round's training and gate, and rounds 183-184 were about 31 min apart (log 11:59 and 12:30 UTC), so 27 min still fits. Check it in the first rounds: if self-play ends after training, drop to 3,000 games and a 36-round window.

## E78 — live-play: robber guard, no robber on our own pips when a clean hex blocks as much (live-play, Oct 21, 9:25 AM Toronto; LIVE on Vasos5306 since Oct 22, 7:03 AM)
- **Trigger:** E75 follow-up (live-loss, 9:35 AM note): at 2100+ the bot puts the robber on its own pips while a hex with the opponent's pips and none of ours is open on 15.5% of its robber moves, people 5.2%; it takes the best legal hex 48.3% vs 60.7%.
- **What:** a root filter on the search (catanatron/colonist/robber_policy.py, like the endgame filter): at a robber move, drop every hex holding our pips whose opponent pips are no more than the best clean hex's (a hex with opponent pips and none of ours). Everything else is kept, and the search still chooses among what is left. In 1v1 every hex touching the opponent steals the same, so a dropped move only gave up income. Off by default; on for one account with ROBBER_FILTER=1 in its env file (or bridge --robber-filter). Captures record robber_filter "own_pips_v1".
- **Before live:** measure on live-loss's failure set (the clearest self-block positions): how many the guard changes, and the search's own value for the dropped move against the move it plays instead. If the search rates the dropped moves clearly higher in many positions, look before deploying.
- **Live rule (when approved, after the opening-clock decision; one live experiment at a time):** one account on its own VM first. Safety at 50 games: no stop, no robber move lost on time. Verdict at 300 games: win rate and rating against the control accounts; expected gain is small (about 15% of robber moves, about 1 card a game), so 300 games mostly rules out harm.
- **Revert:** delete ROBBER_FILTER from the env file, safe_restart_account.sh between games.
- **Oct 21, 10:00 AM: pre-live check on the failure set (live-loss).** robber_set.jsonl, 100 positions, runs/g1/best.npz (round 182), 200 sims, same seeds with the filter off and on (scratch/live-loss/e78_check.py `run NET SIMS [W]`, `table`; rows in data/orchestrator/live-loss/e78_check.jsonl). The filter keeps 9.8 of 18 legal hexes on average and leaves 0 of the set's "bad" hexes (it drops all of them, by construction, and only them). Off: good 24 [17,33], bad 50, other 26 (reproduces the 9:50 AM score). On: good 52% [42,62], bad 0, other 48. Pick changed in 54/100. The unfiltered search's Q for its own pick minus its Q for the filtered pick (both visited in all 54): median +0.020, mean +0.022, p90 +0.061; above 0.05 in 9, above 0.10 in 3. Same noisy gap as the dump (no context separated the bad-ahead cases). Verdict: no reason found to hold it back; the live 300-game test decides.
- **Oct 21, 10:12 AM: the same set at a live-like budget (live-play).** robber_set.py score runs/g1/best.npz (file of 11:57 AM UTC = 7:57 AM Toronto) 1600 sims, no noise: good 20% [12, 28], bad 54% [44, 64] (200 sims: 24 / 50). Q best bad minus best good, all 100 visited: median +0.009; bad ahead by >0.05 in 15, good ahead by >0.05 in 1. Reading: 8x the search does not fix the self-block (it gets slightly more common) and the two kinds of hex converge to about the same value. More clock at robber moves is no substitute for the guard; the guard gives up at most about 1 point of the net's own win estimate in a few positions. Live robber moves get up to 5 s (cap 4,800 sims, about 2,000-4,000 bought).
- **Oct 21, 11:00 AM: knight timing (live-loss, 1,628 games vs 2100+).** No second fault to add to the guard: robbed with a knight playable, the bot lets the robber stay unanswered only 9% [8, 11] of the time. It re-robs with its own knights more than people (9-13% of its robber moves vs 4-5%), and each person robber placement costs it about 20% more income than each of its own costs the person (27.6 vs 22.8 pip-rolls), which fits the self-block finding. E78's scope stays as built (findings.md 'Oct 21, 11:00 AM').
- **Oct 21, 9:50 PM: approved by the lead, SCHEDULED for Oct 22 at the first game end after 7 AM.** Account: Vasos5306 (bot8, bot VM 3, best net). Control: wifedat (bot12, same VM, same net, so speed and opponents match). Not on Dilly6283 (kept for E61's rerun Oct 23). Switch: add ROBBER_FILTER=1 to bot8.env (backup bot8.env.bak-20261022-e78), safe_restart_account.sh bot8 between games, then check that captures carry robber_filter own_pips_v1. Checked 9:45 PM: the code is on VM 3 (bridge.py has the flag); bot8 games.jsonl 267 lines, bot12 121. Safety at 50 games: no stop, no robber move lost on time. Verdict at 300 games: win rate against wifedat and against Vasos5306's own record on the best net, same band; plus robber moves changed per game (own-pips hex avoided) counted from the captures, since the win-rate signal is small. A new tally segment starts at the switch. Revert: delete the line, restart between games.
- **Oct 22, 12:15 AM: baseline for the changed-move count (live-play).** Replayed every ranked capture since Oct 19, 8 PM (scratch/live-play/e78_robber.py ACCOUNT START [END]): the bot's robber move is one the guard would drop on 9.4% of Vasos5306's moves (228 of 2,432, in 102 of 254 games) and 8.9% of wifedat's (100 of 1,121, in 49 of 127 games), all opponent bands. That is about 0.9 changed moves a game. After the switch Vasos5306 should read 0%; any hit means the guard is not on.

## E79 — research: does the needless robber self-block cost games when played out? (research, Oct 21, 10:10 AM Toronto; PROPOSED, built, waits for fleet time)
- **Trigger:** training's note (Oct 21, 9:59 AM): on live-loss's robber failure set the self-block rate is flat from round 13 to round 182, and every net's value slightly prefers the self-block (median +0.013 to +0.026 Q). Is the value net wrong here, or does the self-block really cost little?
- **Hypothesis:** the clean hex wins clearly more when played out, so the value net misses a real cost (a target or data question for training). If the cost is near zero against itself but larger against the people model, self-play simply does not punish it, and only a guard (E78) or people-like data helps.
- **Method:** data/orchestrator/scratch/research/e79_starts.py turns each of the 100 failure-set positions into two starts: the log up to the bot's live robber move as played (bad), and the same with the robber moved to the first good hex (same victim, same stolen card). 99 pairs (1 skipped: the live move stole nothing). 20 copies each, so line j has the same dice in both files (e71_late.py seeds by line). Arms, best net (runs/g1/best.npz) at 200 sims in the bot's chair: (a) against itself, (b) against the people model; 1,980 games per hex per arm, 7,920 in all. Paired result: e79_pair.py NAME (good minus bad, bootstrap by position).
- **Verdict:** clean hex ahead by 2+ points with the 95% range above zero: the value net misses a real cost; research proposes a training-side fix (e.g. a robber-income auxiliary or upweighting robber positions). Under 2 points: the cost is small; E78's guard is enough. Compare against the net's own estimate (Q +0.02 for the bad hex, about 1 point the other way).
- **Smoke (Oct 21, 10:08 AM):** 4 games per arm locally, both arms replay and finish (about 200 s per 4 games on 4 processes).
- **Cost:** about 7,900 fleet games at low priority (E71's 2,000-game arms took 5-13 min), 20-50 min of fleet time. Nothing live. **Revert:** nothing deployed.
- **Run (when the lead OKs):** `.venv/bin/python data/orchestrator/scratch/research/e71_late.py ../e79/self_bad Z:/home/owen/colonist-ai/runs/g1/best.npz:200 Z:/home/owen/colonist-ai/runs/g1/best.npz:200 --starts ../e79/starts_bad`, then self_good with starts_good, then people_bad/people_good with B = people; then `e79_pair.py self` and `e79_pair.py people`.
- **RUNNING (Oct 22, 3:13 PM Toronto)** on the fleet time the lead granted Oct 21 (6:35 PM report). Net pinned to round 208 (best.npz since Oct 22, same md5), not best.npz, so a promotion can't split the arms. Job jobs/e79-run.sh via launch.py e79 (pid 2331798, log jobs/e79.log); games in e79/{self,people}_{bad,good}.jsonl, lines in e71/results.txt, paired results at the end of e79.log.
- **CLOSED, Matches DONE (Oct 22, 3:13-3:53 PM Toronto; research).** 1,980 games per arm, round 208 at 200 sims in the bot's chair, 99 positions x 20 dice. Bot's chair wins: vs itself bad hex 51.4%, clean hex 53.1%; vs the people model bad 52.3%, clean 54.3% (each ± 2.2). Paired, clean minus bad (bootstrap by position): **vs itself +1.7 points (95% +0.5 to +3.0); vs people model +2.0 (+0.6 to +3.5).**
  - **Verdict: a real but small cost, at the line, not over it.** Both ranges are above zero, so the self-block does cost games; self-play's +1.7 is under the 2-point line and the people model's +2.0 sits on it. The net's value leans about 1 point toward the bad hex, so it misjudges these moves by about 3 points, but only on robber moves of this kind, a few a game at most.
  - **Self vs people:** +1.7 vs +2.0, the same within noise. Self-play punishes the self-block as much as people do, so the signal is in the training data already; no people-like data is needed for it. A training-side fix (robber auxiliary, upweighting) is not justified by this size; E78's guard is the right tool. If E78 shows a live gain, it confirms this.
  - Script: scratch/research/e79_pair.py; games in data/orchestrator/e79/*.jsonl. **Revert:** nothing deployed.

## E80 — Opening clock: 40 s per opening settlement on Dilly6283 (bot3) (owner's request Oct 21, 10:16 AM; lead's approval 10:20 AM; live-play)
- **Hypothesis:** on the shared 2-core VM the 20 s opening settlement reaches only about 11,000 of its 20,000 simulations. Giving it 40 s (of colonist's 60 s) lets it reach the cap, and the opening sets the whole game's income, so this is where extra thinking should pay most.
- **Change:** bot3.env on bot VM 2 (ubuntu@3.18.220.56): `THINK_BUDGETS=BUILD_INITIAL_SETTLEMENT=0.7:40:8.3333` (share 0.7 of the clock, cap 40 s, 8.3333 x 2400 = 20,000 simulations cap, unchanged). Standard is (0.4, 20 s, 8.3333). Everything else as before (round 73 pinned, SIMULATIONS 2400). Backup bot3.env.bak-20261021-e80. Restart requested 10:18 AM Toronto, waits for the current game to end; start stamp = the restart (bridge.log line "think budgets from THINK_BUDGETS: {'BUILD_INITIAL_SETTLEMENT': (0.7, 40.0, 8.3333)}").

The win-rate effect is likely under 1-2 points, so (1) and (2) are the readings this week and (3) collects.

The last 16 opening settlements on 20 s: 5,000 to 18,670 simulations, median about 13,000, every one cut at 20.2-23.9 s. Dilly6283 on the standard clock before the switch (12:48:22 to 14:26:41 UTC): 4-4, too few to read.

Not E80's doing.** The browser extension reconnected twice right as the game started (bridge.log line ~169540), so the bridge had no timer reading and fell back to a plain search (think_budget with remaining None). The stale connection then closed on a keepalive timeout; the game carried on (its road came next). Now replayed frames set the timer from the time the page got them (same machine, same clock). Test: `test_game_start_snapshot_sets_the_first_settlements_timer` (replayed start). Synced to the bot VMs; bot3 and bot10 safe-restart requested at 11:12 AM; it takes effect when the current game ends. The other accounts take it at their next restart. Tallies of opening simulations count games after that restart.
- **Oct 21, 11:15 AM Toronto: E80 is 4-0 (games after the start stamp), game 5 in progress.** Openings with a timer: 20,000/20,000, 20,000 (28.1 s), 19,818 (40.5 s)/20,000, 20,000 (26.3 s), and game 5 15,348 (40.5 s)/20,000 (30.0 s). So on the shared VM a settlement sometimes still hits the 40 s cap before 20,000; the longest so far is 40.5 s of 60 s, no time trouble. bot3 and bot10 restarts with the fix still waiting for their games to end.
